            - generic [ref=e64]:
              - heading "Exploitation of Hetero- and Phototrophic Metabolic Modules for Redox-Intensive Whole-Cell Biocatalysis" [level=1] [ref=e66]
              - generic [ref=e67]:
                - link "Eleni Theodosiou" [ref=e68] [cursor=pointer]:
                  - /url: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Theodosiou%20E"[Author]
                - superscript [ref=e69]: "1"
                - text: ","
                - link "Adrian Tüllinghoff" [ref=e70] [cursor=pointer]:
                  - /url: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="T%C3%BCllinghoff%20A"[Author]
                - superscript [ref=e71]: "2"
                - text: ","
                - link "Jörg Toepel" [ref=e72] [cursor=pointer]:
                  - /url: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Toepel%20J"[Author]
                - superscript [ref=e73]: "2"
                - text: ","
                - link "Bruno Bühler" [ref=e74] [cursor=pointer]:
                  - /url: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="B%C3%BChler%20B"[Author]
                - superscript [ref=e75]: 2,
                - superscript [ref=e76]: "*"
              - list [ref=e77]:
                - listitem [ref=e78]:
                  - button "▸ Author information" [ref=e79] [cursor=pointer]
                - listitem [ref=e80]:
                  - button "▸ Article notes" [ref=e81] [cursor=pointer]
                - listitem [ref=e82]:
                  - button "▸ Copyright and License information" [ref=e83] [cursor=pointer]
              - generic: External, opens in a new tab. External, opens in a new tab. External, opens in a new tab.
              - generic [ref=e84]:
                - text: "PMCID: PMC9043136 PMID:"
                - link "35497353" [ref=e85] [cursor=pointer]:
                  - /url: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35497353/
          - region "Article content" [ref=e86]:
            - generic [ref=e87]:
              - generic [ref=e88]:
                - heading "Abstract" [level=2] [ref=e89]: Abstract
                - paragraph [ref=e90]: The successful realization of a sustainable manufacturing bioprocess and the maximization of its production potential and capacity are the main concerns of a bioprocess engineer. A main step towards this endeavor is the development of an efficient biocatalyst. Isolated enzyme(s), microbial cells, or (immobilized) formulations thereof can serve as biocatalysts. Living cells feature, beside active enzymes, metabolic modules that can be exploited to support energy-dependent and multi-step enzyme-catalyzed reactions. Metabolism can sustainably supply necessary cofactors or cosubstrates at the expense of readily available and cheap resources, rendering external addition of costly cosubstrates unnecessary. However, for the development of an efficient whole-cell biocatalyst, in depth comprehension of metabolic modules and their interconnection with cell growth, maintenance, and product formation is indispensable. In order to maximize the flux through biosynthetic reactions and pathways to an industrially relevant product and respective key performance indices (i.e., titer, yield, and productivity), existing metabolic modules can be redesigned and/or novel artificial ones established. This review focuses on whole-cell bioconversions that are coupled to heterotrophic or phototrophic metabolism and discusses metabolic engineering efforts aiming at 1) increasing regeneration and supply of redox equivalents, such as NAD(P/H), 2) blocking competing fluxes, and 3) increasing the availability of metabolites serving as (co)substrates of desired biosynthetic routes.
                - paragraph [ref=e92]:
                  - strong [ref=e93]: "Keywords:"
                  - text: whole-cell redox biocatalysis, central metabolism, TCA cycle, metabolic engineering, cyanobacteria, redox balance
              - generic [ref=e94]:
                - heading "1 Introduction" [level=2] [ref=e95]: 1 Introduction
                - paragraph [ref=e96]:
                  - text: Living microbial cells have at their disposal active enzymes and metabolic pathways that allow the catalysis of synthetically interesting reactions in an economically feasible way and, in many cases, the simultaneous
                  - emphasis [ref=e97]: in situ
                  - text: regeneration of required cofactors or cosubstrates. They can theoretically produce all the metabolites present in their metabolic network from simple carbon sources such as sugars, organic acids, or even CO
                  - subscript [ref=e98]: "2"
                  - text: . If naturally occurring microorganisms are employed, the production efficiencies typically are rather low (
                  - link "Chae et al., 2017" [ref=e99] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B36"
                  - text: ). For the successful development of robust, productive, and efficient whole-cell biocatalysts, whole-cell systems have to be considered in their entire complexity (
                  - link "Lee et al., 2013" [ref=e100] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B112"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Schrewe et al., 2013" [ref=e101] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B176"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Ladkau et al., 2014" [ref=e102] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B103"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Volmer et al., 2015" [ref=e103] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B210"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Kadisch et al., 2017" [ref=e104] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B81"
                  - text: ). Cell physiology is intrinsically optimized to secure growth and not the envisaged bioconversion. The maximization of the crucial process performance parameters titer, yield, and productivity thus typically requires the engineering of metabolic machineries
                  - emphasis [ref=e105]: via
                  - text: the redesign of existing metabolic pathways and/or the introduction of novel/artificial ones (
                  - link "Chae et al., 2017" [ref=e106] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B36"
                  - text: ).
                - paragraph [ref=e107]:
                  - text: The present review focuses on the exploitation and engineering of modules of hetero- and phototrophic metabolism as well as artificial modules for redox-intensive production schemes. In heterotrophs, the central carbon metabolism provides carbon building blocks as well as redox power and energy by making use of simple organic molecules such as sugars and thus constitutes the hub for nearly all catabolic and anabolic processes. It includes the Embden–Meyerhof–Parnas pathway (EMPP), the Entner-Doudoroff pathway (EDP), the pentose phosphate pathway (PPP), as well as the tricarboxylic acid (TCA) cycle (
                  - link "Macnab, 1990" [ref=e108] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B136"
                  - text: ), with few individual variations depending on the ecological niche in which the organism lives (
                  - link "Sudarsan et al., 2014" [ref=e109] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B191"
                  - text: ). During aerobic growth, a maximum of 24 reducing equivalents (electrons) in the form of 12 molecules of NADH, NADPH, or FADH
                  - subscript [ref=e110]: "2"
                  - text: is theoretically obtained per molecule of glucose oxidized to CO
                  - subscript [ref=e111]: "2"
                  - text: (
                  - link "Blank et al., 2010" [ref=e112] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B22"
                  - text: ). Their transfer to O
                  - subscript [ref=e113]: "2"
                  - emphasis [ref=e114]: via
                  - text: the respiratory electron transport chain then generates the majority of the metabolic energy. In contrast, photoautotrophic metabolism relies on differing resources for carbon, electrons, and energy, i.e., CO
                  - subscript [ref=e115]: "2"
                  - text: ", water, and light, respectively. The light reactions of photosynthesis gather electrons from water using light energy (photosynthetic water oxidation), generate a proton gradient"
                  - emphasis [ref=e116]: via
                  - text: the photosynthetic electron transport chain (PETC), and thereby regenerate NADPH and ATP to fuel CO
                  - subscript [ref=e117]: "2"
                  - text: fixation
                  - emphasis [ref=e118]: via
                  - text: the Calvin-Benson-Bassham cycle (
                  - link "Lassen et al., 2014a" [ref=e119] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B107"
                  - text: ). The latter feeds glycderaldehyde-3-phosphate (G3P) into gluconeogenesis and lower glycolysis, thereby connecting to EMPP, EDP, and PPP and indirectly also the TCA cycle. In both heterotrophs and photoautotrophs, the central carbon metabolism produces biomass precursors (
                  - link "Chen et al., 2016" [ref=e120] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B39"
                  - text: ) and is highly connected to redox and energy metabolism (
                  - link "Liao et al., 2016" [ref=e121] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B125"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Wang et al., 2017" [ref=e122] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B212"
                  - text: ).
                - paragraph [ref=e123]:
                  - text: Phototrophic organisms are increasingly attracting attention as hosts for the sustainable production of C-based products, as they can rely on light, CO
                  - subscript [ref=e124]: "2"
                  - text: ", and water for growth and product formation. This allows biocatalyst production, regeneration, and operation at minimal economical and ecological costs in overall carbon neutral and land-saving processes. Recent results show the high potential of cyanobacteria, green algae, and other phototrophs to produce biomass ("
                  - link "Tsuzuki et al., 2019" [ref=e125] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B199"
                  - text: ), biofuels (
                  - link "Arias et al., 2021" [ref=e126] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B8"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Kato et al., 2022" [ref=e127] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B87"
                  - text: ), chemicals (
                  - link "Vavitsas et al., 2021" [ref=e128] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B205"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Jaiswal et al., 2022" [ref=e129] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B75"
                  - text: ), and high value compounds (
                  - link "Tanvir et al., 2021" [ref=e130] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B192"
                  - text: ). Respective advantages and disadvantages are intensely discussed, and new strategies have been developed to overcome limitations in carbon fixation (
                  - link "Batista-Silva et al., 2020" [ref=e131] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B15"
                  - text: ). In particular, electron consuming reactions are prone to benefit from electrons derived from photosynthetic water oxidation, with H
                  - subscript [ref=e132]: "2"
                  - text: formation as a prominent example (
                  - link "Appel et al., 2020" [ref=e133] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B7"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Lupacchini et al., 2021" [ref=e134] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B135"
                  - text: ). Maximal electron transfer rates in phototrophs illustrate their high potential to fuel biocatalytic reactions (
                  - link "Grund et al., 2019" [ref=e135] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B58"
                  - text: ). Additionally, the photosynthesis product O
                  - subscript [ref=e136]: "2"
                  - text: can be utilized to support O
                  - subscript [ref=e137]: "2"
                  - text: "-dependent reactions ("
                  - link "Hoschek et al., 2017" [ref=e138] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B67"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Hoschek et al., 2018" [ref=e139] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B69"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Tüllinghoff et al., 2022" [ref=e140] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B200"
                  - text: ), with the potential to overcome O
                  - subscript [ref=e141]: "2"
                  - text: limitation, a major bottleneck in many processes based on heterotrophs (
                  - link "Baldwin and Woodley, 2006" [ref=e142] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B11"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Ouyang et al., 2018" [ref=e143] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B151"
                  - text: ). However, C- (
                  - link "Angermayr et al., 2015" [ref=e144] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B4"
                  - text: ) and e
                  - superscript [ref=e145]: −
                  - text: "-based ("
                  - link "Jodlbauer et al., 2021" [ref=e146] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B78"
                  - text: ) processes with phototrophic organisms are still limited by several factors, ranging from the lack of advanced molecular biology tools and a poor understanding of the cellar metabolism and its regulation to the lack of bioreactor systems enabling high cell density cultivation (
                  - link "Yu et al., 2013" [ref=e147] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B226"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Till et al., 2020" [ref=e148] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B197"
                  - text: ). Generally heterotrophic organisms can still be considered the favored hosts for most production processes due to 1) higher growth rates, 2) a considerably deeper understanding of metabolic and regulatory networks, 3) a more expanded molecular biology tool box, 4) a very active metabolism, i.e., a high glucose consumption rate, and 5) the high cell density cultivation options. These advantages allow elaborate processes like
                  - emphasis [ref=e149]: in vivo
                  - text: cascades (
                  - link "Ladkau et al., 2014" [ref=e150] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B103"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Ladkau et al., 2016" [ref=e151] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B102"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Woo et al., 2018" [ref=e152] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B220"
                  - text: ) and the production of fine and bulk chemicals at industrial scale. However, as stated above, phototrophic organisms have a high potential as hosts for electron demanding reactions, making glucose or other reduced C-compounds for the regeneration of reduction equivalent dispensable. Thus, bioprocesses based on phototrophs profit from low land use demands. Whereas heterotrophic hosts require—beside a low area demand for the bioreactor plants—arable land for the rather area-inefficient production of glucose or other organic substrates, phototrophs show comparably high areal productivity and can also be applied on non-arable land and buildings (
                  - link "Brandenburg et al., 2021" [ref=e153] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B27"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Fernández et al., 2021" [ref=e154] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B54"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Schipper et al., 2021" [ref=e155] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B175"
                  - text: ). As long as glucose remains a rather cheap resource, it will be difficult for photobiotechnology to compete; but facing a growing global population with an increasing demand for food, energy, and chemicals, photobiotechnology can be attributed a high future potential making research on respective molecular biology tools, metabolic and regulatory modules, and high cell density cultivation highly valuable.
                - paragraph [ref=e156]:
                  - text: Since the synthesis of most target products make use of metabolic modules either providing carbon or energy, maximizing respective fluxes is pivotal. This requires knowledge on structure and function of the metabolic network as well as its response upon environmental or genetic perturbations (
                  - link "Jahan et al., 2016" [ref=e157] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B73"
                  - text: ;
                  - link "Aslan et al., 2017" [ref=e158] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B9"
                  - text: ). In case of redox-dependent production systems, the rate of redox cofactor regeneration is intrinsically linked with the entirety of metabolic modules producing and consuming redox cofactors (
                  - link "Blank et al., 2010" [ref=e159] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B22"
                  - text: ) and thus depends on the chosen microbial production system. Whereas heterotrophs rely on the central carbon metabolism with a bias for NADH over NADPH supply, phototrophs involve the light reaction as main module with a bias for NADPH instead of NADH supply, both with respective challenges in redox balancing. In phototrophs, the light reactions often generate more reducing power than their primary metabolism needs, i.e., their metabolism is sink-limited, so that excess reducing power can be exploited for light-driven biosyntheses (
                  - link "Lassen et al., 2014a" [ref=e160] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B107"
                  - text: ). Metabolic cofactor regeneration can become limiting, when the target reaction runs at high rates and has to compete with NAD(P)H demands for maintenance and biomass formation. It is obvious that the bioprocess objective to maximize target product formation is not in line with the natural objective of a cell to exploit its redox and energy metabolism for optimized growth and maintenance (
                  - link "Schrewe et al., 2013" [ref=e161] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B176"
                  - text: ). To overcome metabolic and cofactor imbalances that impede whole-cell biocatalyst performance, various engineering strategies have been employed, which will be summarized and discussed in this review. These strategies either aim at increasing regeneration and supply of redox equivalents or at blocking competing fluxes. Thereby, heterotrophic, phototrophic, and introduced modules are considered and discussed regarding their potential to fuel whole-cell redox biocatalysis, including strategies to improve them.
                - generic [ref=e162]:
                  - heading "2 Rerouting Electron Transfer Using Enzymatic Modules" [level=3] [ref=e163]
                  - paragraph [ref=e164]:
                    - text: In microbial metabolism, redox cofactors serve as electron carriers providing the cell with the reducing power needed in energy and/or anabolic metabolism. Furthermore, they play critical roles in maintaining intracellular redox homeostasis and are key players for the production of many, fuels, chemicals, and pharmaceuticals
                    - emphasis [ref=e165]: via
                    - text: redox biocatalysis (
                    - link "Duetz et al., 2001" [ref=e166] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B46"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Zhao and van der Donk, 2003" [ref=e167] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B236"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Park, 2007" [ref=e168] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B153"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Schroer et al., 2009" [ref=e169] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B177"
                    - text: ). Their essential role in host metabolism and the complexity and flexibility of their turnover give metabolism-based cofactor regeneration a high potential, but make respective analyses and metabolic engineering towards efficient microbial cell factories a complex task (
                    - link "Blank et al., 2008" [ref=e170] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B23"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Holm et al., 2010" [ref=e171] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B65"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Lee et al., 2013" [ref=e172] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B112"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e173]:
                    - text: Redox balancing is pivotal for living cells and is affected by any respective manipulation. An important aspect is the different roles, which NAD(H) and NADP(H) play in heterotrophic and phototrophic cells. In the latter, NADPH constitutes a main product of the photosynthetic light reaction, the electron donor for CO
                    - subscript [ref=e174]: "2"
                    - text: fixation, and thus the central electron carrier under illumination (
                    - link "Ishikawa and Kawai-Yamada, 2019" [ref=e175] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B71"
                    - text: ). This changes in the dark, when maintenance metabolism mainly relies on NADH and respiration. In heterotrophs, NADH is the main electron carrier for catabolism and NADPH for anabolism. Both, hetero- and phototrophs can balance pool sizes and reroute electrons between different electron carriers, involving transhydrogenases and NAD kinases (
                    - link "Figure 1" [ref=e176] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F1"
                    - text: ). The different roles of transhydrogenases and NAD kinases in hetero- and phototrophs, as well as the usage of heterologous NAD(P)H regeneration systems will be discussed below. Biotechnological approaches based on living cells have to consider not only the electron donor dependency of synthetic reactions and pathways, but also the presence of competing pathways and the cellular responses upon electron drain.
                  - 'figure "Interconversion and regeneration of redox equivalents via reactions that are not directly coupled to the central carbon metabolism: NAD+ kinase (EC 2.7.1.23); NADH kinase (EC 2.7.1.86), NADP+ phosphatase (EC 2.7.1.23); NAD+-dependent dehydrogenase (EC:1.12.1.2); NADP(H)+-dependent dehydrogenases (EC 1.12.1.3); energy-independent soluble pyridine nucleotide transhydrogenase (UdhA, EC 1.6.1.1); energy-dependent proton translocating membrane-bound pyridine nucleotide transhydrogenase (PntAB, EC 1.6.1.2); ferredoxin:NADP+ oxidoreductase (FNR, EC 1.18.1.2)." [ref=e177]':
                    - heading "FIGURE 1." [level=4] [ref=e178]
                    - paragraph [ref=e179]:
                      - link "FIGURE 1 🔍" [ref=e180] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=9043136_fbioe-10-855715-g001.jpg
                        - img "FIGURE 1" [ref=e181]
                        - text: 🔍
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e183] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/F1/
                    - paragraph [ref=e185]:
                      - text: "Interconversion and regeneration of redox equivalents via reactions that are not directly coupled to the central carbon metabolism: NAD"
                      - superscript [ref=e186]: +
                      - text: kinase (EC 2.7.1.23); NADH kinase (EC 2.7.1.86), NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e187]: +
                      - text: phosphatase (EC 2.7.1.23); NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e188]: +
                      - text: "-dependent dehydrogenase (EC:1.12.1.2); NADP(H)"
                      - superscript [ref=e189]: +
                      - text: "-dependent dehydrogenases (EC 1.12.1.3); energy-independent soluble pyridine nucleotide transhydrogenase (UdhA, EC 1.6.1.1); energy-dependent proton translocating membrane-bound pyridine nucleotide transhydrogenase (PntAB, EC 1.6.1.2); ferredoxin:NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e190]: +
                      - text: oxidoreductase (FNR, EC 1.18.1.2).
                - generic [ref=e191]:
                  - heading "2.1 Engineering by Means of NAD(P/H) Transhydrogenases" [level=3] [ref=e192]
                  - paragraph [ref=e193]:
                    - text: The function of NAD(P/H) transhydrogenases differs among organisms, especially between phototrophic and heterotrophic bacteria, and is not completely understood, yet. The transhydrogenase reaction (NADH + NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e194]: +
                    - text: ↔ NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e195]: +
                    - text: + NADPH) is catalyzed by the membrane-bound, proton-translocating PntAB (EC 1.6.1.2) or the soluble, energy-independent UdhA (EC 1.6.1.1) (
                    - link "Sauer et al., 2004" [ref=e196] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B172"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Pedersen et al., 2008" [ref=e197] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B156"
                    - text: ). Up to now, only Enterobacteriaceae are known to contain both enzymes; all other microbes contain either one or none. Cyanobacteria harbor the genes for PntAB only, which was found to be particularly important for NADPH production under low-light mixotrophic conditions (
                    - link "Kämäräinen et al., 2017" [ref=e198] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B83"
                    - text: ). Using mutant
                    - emphasis [ref=e199]: Escherichia coli
                    - text: strains and metabolic flux analysis (MFA), Sauer et al. (
                    - link "Sauer et al., 2004" [ref=e200] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B172"
                    - text: ) identified PntAB as major source of NADPH and reported that it provides 35–45% of the NADPH required during standard aerobic batch growth on glucose, whereas PPP and isocitrate dehydrogenase contributed 35–45% and 20–25%, respectively. On the other hand, hydride transfer from NADPH to NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e201]: +
                    - text: by UdhA was found to be essential for growth under metabolic conditions with excess NADPH formation, such as growth on acetate or with inactivated glucose-6-phosphate isomerase (PGI).
                    - link "Canonaco et al. (2001)" [ref=e202] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B33"
                    - text: demonstrated that, in a Δ
                    - emphasis [ref=e203]: pgi
                    - text: mutant with the PPP as primary route of glucose catabolism, UdhA activity could efficiently restore the redox balance and improve the growth rate by 25%. They concluded that, in prokaryotes, PntAB catalyzes energy- and NADH-dependent NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e204]: +
                    - text: reduction at low intracellular NADPH levels, whereas UdhA oxidizes NADPH at high intracellular NADPH levels (
                    - link "Sauer et al., 2004" [ref=e205] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B172"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e206]:
                    - text: The function of the transhydrogenases in phototrophic prokaryotes is thought to be similar, maintaining redox homeostasis under imbalanced conditions. Normally, PntAB activity can be neglected during photoautotrophic growth (
                    - link "Kämäräinen et al., 2017" [ref=e207] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B83"
                    - text: ). In
                    - emphasis [ref=e208]: Synechocystis
                    - text: sp. PCC 6803, however,
                    - emphasis [ref=e209]: pntA
                    - text: transcription was shown to be high under nitrogen and phosphate limitation (
                    - link "Kopf et al., 2014" [ref=e210] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B99"
                    - text: ), conditions known to involve an unbalanced NADH/NADPH ratio, suggesting that PntAB plays an important role for redox homeostasis under such conditions. Similarly, it was found that mixotrophic growth, with an enhanced NADH level, depends on PntAB in cyanobacteria (
                    - link "Kämäräinen et al., 2017" [ref=e211] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B83"
                    - text: ). A
                    - emphasis [ref=e212]: pntAB
                    - text: "-deficient mutant exhibited growth defects under low-light conditions and day-night rhythm in the presence of glucose. However, the redox state in terms of the NADPH/NADP ratio also appears to be subject of large diurnal variation and to play an important role in respective global regulation ("
                    - link "Saha et al., 2016" [ref=e213] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B169"
                    - text: ). Further studies on the function of transhydrogenases in phototrophs are missing.
                  - paragraph [ref=e214]:
                    - text: The transhydrogenase systems constitute promising engineering targets to enhance NAD(P)H-dependent biotransformations both in phototrophs and heterotrophs. Because membrane proteins are usually considered more tedious to be heterologously expressed, soluble UdhA is more commonly used in metabolic engineering. Such engineering was successfully employed for the syntheses of several industrially useful compounds. In the study of
                    - link "Xu et al. (2019)" [ref=e215] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B223"
                    - text: ", a highly NADPH-demanding squalene synthesis pathway was established in"
                    - emphasis [ref=e216]: E. coli
                    - text: to create a squalene-producing bacterial strain. Thereby,
                    - emphasis [ref=e217]: udhA
                    - text: was overexpressed and obviously enabled an improved intracellular NADPH/NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e218]: +
                    - text: ratio, resulting in a 59% increase in squalene titer. For (S)-2-chloropropionate production from 2-chloroacrylate catalyzed by the NADPH-dependent 2-haloacrylate reductase,
                    - link "Jan et al. (2013)" [ref=e219] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B76"
                    - text: found that the presence of UdhA increases product yield and NADPH availability while the presence of PntAB has the opposite effect. UdhA also conveyed an increase in polyhydroxybutyrate (PHB) productivity and yield in engineered
                    - emphasis [ref=e220]: E. coli
                    - text: (
                    - link "Sanchez et al., 2006" [ref=e221] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B170"
                    - text: ). Coexpression of native
                    - emphasis [ref=e222]: udh
                    - text: A from
                    - emphasis [ref=e223]: E. coli
                    - text: together with the
                    - emphasis [ref=e224]: phb
                    - text: operon from
                    - emphasis [ref=e225]: Alcaligenes eutrophus
                    - text: H16 from high copy plasmids resulted in an increase in PHB yield from 49 to 66% of total cell dry weight and an increase in final concentration from 3.52 to 6.42 g L
                    - superscript [ref=e226]: −1
                    - text: compared to the control strain expressing only the
                    - emphasis [ref=e227]: phb
                    - text: operon. These results are somewhat in contrast to those reported by Sauer et al. for redox balancing during growth and indicate that UdhA not only oxidizes NADPH at high NADPH levels, but also contributes to NADPH formation at low NADPH levels caused by a strong NADPH sink (
                    - link "Schroer et al., 2009" [ref=e228] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B177"
                    - text: ). Using a thymidine-overproducing
                    - emphasis [ref=e229]: E. coli
                    - text: strain, the NADPH availability increased by disrupting PGI and overexpressing
                    - emphasis [ref=e230]: nadK
                    - text: (NAD kinase) or
                    - emphasis [ref=e231]: udhA
                    - text: . In chemostat cultures, the NADPH/NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e232]: +
                    - text: ratios at steady state correlated positively with thymidine yields (
                    - link "Lee et al., 2010" [ref=e233] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B109"
                    - text: ). Fatty acid production also depends on NADPH and has been reported to decrease by 88.8% upon deletion of
                    - emphasis [ref=e234]: pntB
                    - text: and
                    - emphasis [ref=e235]: udhA
                    - text: in
                    - emphasis [ref=e236]: E. coli
                    - text: . Whereas the deactivation of the
                    - emphasis [ref=e237]: zwf
                    - text: gene decreased production, fatty acid biosynthesis in the
                    - emphasis [ref=e238]: zwf
                    - text: mutant was recovered upon co‐expression of
                    - emphasis [ref=e239]: pntAB
                    - text: and
                    - emphasis [ref=e240]: nadK
                    - text: (
                    - link "Li et al., 2018" [ref=e241] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B119"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e242]:
                    - text: In cyanobacteria, the production of lactic acid is the first example of how the heterologous coexpression of
                    - emphasis [ref=e243]: udhA
                    - text: (e.g., from
                    - emphasis [ref=e244]: E. coli
                    - text: ","
                    - emphasis [ref=e245]: B. subtilis
                    - text: ) together with lactate dehydrogenase (e.g., from
                    - emphasis [ref=e246]: E. coli, P. aeruginosa
                    - text: ) improves the production due to enhanced NADH supply (
                    - link "Angermayr et al., 2012" [ref=e247] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B5"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Varman et al., 2013" [ref=e248] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B204"
                    - text: ). Whereas overexpression of
                    - emphasis [ref=e249]: E. coli udhA
                    - text: in cyanobacteria improved NADH-dependent production of lactate, phototrophic growth was reduced, probably due to NADPH consumption by UdhA (
                    - link "Niederholtmeyer et al., 2010" [ref=e250] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B147"
                    - text: ). Conversely, overexpression of endogenous
                    - emphasis [ref=e251]: pntAB
                    - text: in
                    - emphasis [ref=e252]: Synechocystis
                    - text: increased the NADPH-dependent production of 3-hydroxypropionic acid without inhibition of phototrophic growth (
                    - link "Wang et al., 2016" [ref=e253] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B214"
                    - text: ). Similarly,
                    - emphasis [ref=e254]: pntAB
                    - text: overexpression was shown to support NADPH-dependent sorbitol production in
                    - emphasis [ref=e255]: Synechocystis
                    - text: sp. PCC 6803 (
                    - link "Chin et al., 2018" [ref=e256] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B42"
                    - text: ). Also here, a rebalancing of NADPH availability obviously was necessary, enabling, together with an increase in the fructose bisphosphatase level, a nearly 27-fold increase in sorbitol titer compared to the initial strain just overexpressing a sorbitol-6-phosphate dehydrogenase gene.
                  - paragraph [ref=e257]:
                    - text: As mentioned above, transhydrogenases are lacking in some biotechnologically interesting microorganisms.
                    - link "Yamauchi et al. (2014)" [ref=e258] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B224"
                    - text: introduced the
                    - emphasis [ref=e259]: E. coli udhA
                    - text: and
                    - emphasis [ref=e260]: pntAB
                    - text: genes into glutamic acid-producing
                    - emphasis [ref=e261]: Corynebacterium glutamicum
                    - text: and examined the metabolic characteristics of the recombinant strains under aerobic and microaerobic conditions. Introduction of the
                    - emphasis [ref=e262]: udh
                    - text: A gene did not cause major metabolic changes under both conditions. However, the introduction of
                    - emphasis [ref=e263]: pnt
                    - text: AB increased the NADH/NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e264]: +
                    - text: ratio under microaerobic conditions and thereby lactic, acetic, and succinic acid formation, pointing towards the potential of PntAB to catalyze a rebalancing towards NADH formation depending on growth conditions. In
                    - emphasis [ref=e265]: Rhodospirillum rubrum
                    - text: ", a purple non-sulfur alphaproteobacterium, the overexpression of"
                    - emphasis [ref=e266]: E. coli pntAB
                    - text: together with
                    - emphasis [ref=e267]: Ralstonia eutropha phaB1
                    - text: ", encoding an NADPH-dependent polyhydroxyalkanoate (PHA)-precursor synthesizing reductase, accumulated poly(3-hydroxybutyrate-co-3-hydroxyvalerate) with a 13-fold higher 3-hydroxyvalerate content compared to the wild type strain ("
                    - link "Heinrich et al., 2015" [ref=e268] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B61"
                    - text: ). The engineered
                    - emphasis [ref=e269]: R. rubrum
                    - text: strain was also able to synthesize this industrially relevant copolymer from CO
                    - subscript [ref=e270]: "2"
                    - text: and CO in syngas. The increased incorporation of 3-hydroxyvalerate was attributed to an excess of the PHA precursor propionyl-CoA, which was generated from aspartate via threonine, thereby consuming two NADPH and producing one NADH, to compensate for PntAB-catalyzed electron transfer from NADH to NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e271]: +
                    - text: . The increased 3-hydroxyvalerate incorporation possibly also involved a shortage of the otherwise preferred PHA precursor acetyl-CoA consumed by the NADH generating TCA cycle (
                    - link "Heinrich et al., 2015" [ref=e272] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B61"
                    - text: ). Obviously, the function of the two types of transhydrogenases (UdhA and PntAB) is not unidirectional and, depending on the conditions and host organism applied, both can support NADPH or NADH supply.
                - generic [ref=e273]:
                  - heading "2.2 Overexpression of NAD+ kinases" [level=3] [ref=e274]:
                    - text: 2.2 Overexpression of NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e275]: +
                    - text: kinases
                  - paragraph [ref=e276]:
                    - text: NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e277]: +
                    - text: kinases (NADKs) catalyze the phosphorylation of NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e278]: +
                    - text: to NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e279]: +
                    - text: using ATP as the phosphoryl donor (
                    - link "Kawai et al., 2001" [ref=e280] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B90"
                    - text: ). These enzymes are key to balance the ratio of NAD(H) to NADP(H). In an engineered thymidine producing
                    - emphasis [ref=e281]: E. coli
                    - text: strain, it was reported that
                    - emphasis [ref=e282]: nadK
                    - text: overexpression resulted in a shift of the NADPH/NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e283]: +
                    - text: ratio from 0.184 to 0.267. However, the overall (NADH + NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e284]: +
                    - text: )/(NADPH + NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e285]: +
                    - text: ) ratio remained constant probably by regulating
                    - emphasis [ref=e286]: pntAB
                    - text: and
                    - emphasis [ref=e287]: udhA
                    - text: expression levels to compensate for the effects of
                    - emphasis [ref=e288]: nad
                    - text: K overexpression (
                    - link "Lee et al., 2009" [ref=e289] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B110"
                    - text: ). The
                    - emphasis [ref=e290]: nad
                    - text: K overexpression in a recombinant
                    - emphasis [ref=e291]: E. coli
                    - text: harboring a polyhydroxybutyrate (PHB) synthesis pathway doubled the overall PHB accumulation in a bioreactor experiment and came along with a 3- to 6-fold increase in NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e292]: +
                    - text: concentration (
                    - link "Li et al., 2009" [ref=e293] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B120"
                    - text: ). The overexpression of the NADK gene
                    - emphasis [ref=e294]: yfjB
                    - text: together with
                    - emphasis [ref=e295]: pntAB
                    - text: in
                    - emphasis [ref=e296]: E. coli
                    - text: was reported to increase NADPH availability and thereby isobutanol production to a larger extent than that of
                    - emphasis [ref=e297]: pntAB
                    - text: alone, whereas
                    - emphasis [ref=e298]: yfjB
                    - text: overexpression alone had no effect (
                    - link "Shi et al., 2013" [ref=e299] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B181"
                    - text: ). This beneficial synergistic effect was more prominent under anaerobic conditions, when NADH constitutes the main electron carrier, PPP and TCA cycle are usually not functional, and transhydrogenases are the only source for NADPH. In general, NADPH-dependent bioproduction routes of thymidine (
                    - link "Lee et al., 2010" [ref=e300] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B109"
                    - text: ), isobutanol (
                    - link "Shi et al., 2013" [ref=e301] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B181"
                    - text: ), PHB (
                    - link "Li et al., 2009" [ref=e302] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B120"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Zhang et al., 2015" [ref=e303] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B230"
                    - text: ), and shikimic acid (
                    - link "Cui et al., 2014" [ref=e304] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B43"
                    - text: ) in
                    - emphasis [ref=e305]: E. coli
                    - text: and of L-arginine (
                    - link "Rahman et al., 2012" [ref=e306] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B164"
                    - text: ) and L-isoleucine (
                    - link "Shi et al., 2012" [ref=e307] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B182"
                    - text: ) in
                    - emphasis [ref=e308]: Corynebacterium
                    - text: species were enhanced via
                    - emphasis [ref=e309]: nadK
                    - text: overexpression alone or combined with genes encoding other NADPH-related enzymes.
                  - paragraph [ref=e310]:
                    - text: In cyanobacteria, NADKs have several functions. Proteome search for NADK homologs of the 72 species registered in CyanoBase revealed 69 species having two typical NADKs classified as Slr0400 type (group 1) and Sll1415 type (group 2) (
                    - link "Ishikawa and Kawai-Yamada, 2019" [ref=e311] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B71"
                    - text: ). It is hypothesized that the two NADK types possess distinct functions in cyanobacteria.
                    - link "Ishikawa et al. (2019)" [ref=e312] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B72"
                    - text: reported that a sll1415-deficient mutant showed a growth-impaired phenotype under conditions that would typically yield photomixotrophy (12-h light/12-h dark cycling), or heterotrophy (light-activated heterotrophic growth, LAHG, with cells in darkness exposed to light for 15 min every 24 h) and that wild type cells exhibited 2.2- and 1.8-fold increased NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e313]: +
                    - text: and NADPH levels, respectively, 96 h after shifting from autotrophic to photomixotrophic conditions. This suggests a key role of group 2 NADKs in photoheterotrophic growth. On the other hand, it is speculated that group 1 NADKs play a key role in suppressing heterotrophic metabolism in
                    - emphasis [ref=e314]: Synechocystis
                    - text: sp. PCC 6803 under photoautotrophic conditions (
                    - link "Ishikawa and Kawai-Yamada, 2019" [ref=e315] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B71"
                    - text: ). A
                    - emphasis [ref=e316]: slr0400
                    - text: "-deficient mutant accumulated NAD"
                    - superscript [ref=e317]: +
                    - text: ", resulting in unsuppressed activity of glycolysis and TCA cycle enzymes ("
                    - link "Ishikawa et al., 2021" [ref=e318] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B70"
                    - text: ). Further research is necessary to clarify the exact role of the two NADK types in cyanobacteria. Effects of NADK gene overexpression and deletion on target product formation have not been investigated, yet.
                - generic [ref=e319]:
                  - heading "2.3 Exploiting Heterologous NAD(P)H Generating Systems" [level=3] [ref=e320]
                  - paragraph [ref=e321]:
                    - text: Apart from the metabolic engineering of intrinsic NAD(P)H generating pathways, studies have focused on exploiting heterologous systems to increase intracellular NAD(P)H availability and enhance the efficiency and productivity of NAD(P)H-dependent whole-cell biotransformations (
                    - link "Figure 2" [ref=e322] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F2"
                    - text: ). Of those, glucose dehydrogenases (GDHs) and formate dehydrogenases (FDHs) are most widely used. The first catalyzes the oxidation of β-D-glucose to β-D-glucono-1,5-lactone with simultaneous reduction of NAD(P)
                    - superscript [ref=e323]: +
                    - text: to NAD(P)H, the latter the NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e324]: +
                    - text: "-dependent oxidation of formate to CO"
                    - subscript [ref=e325]: "2"
                    - text: .
                  - figure [ref=e326]:
                    - heading "FIGURE 2." [level=4] [ref=e327]
                    - paragraph [ref=e328]:
                      - link "FIGURE 2 🔍" [ref=e329] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=9043136_fbioe-10-855715-g002.jpg
                        - img "FIGURE 2" [ref=e330]
                        - text: 🔍
                    - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e332] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: figure/F2/
                    - paragraph [ref=e334]:
                      - text: Overview of the NAD(P)
                      - superscript [ref=e335]: +
                      - text: "/NAD(P)H dependent reactions in central carbon metabolic modules (glycolysis, pentose phosphate pathway, TCA cycle, and Entner-Doudoroff pathway) and heterologous redox cofactor regeneration systems commonly used to fuel redox-intensive production processes. Green arrows and red arrows indicate reactions, which have been upregulated and downregulated/deleted, respectively, to promote/improve redox intensive production schemes. Enzyme names are accompanied by their abbreviations as cited in the text. Respective genes are: the PPP genes"
                      - emphasis [ref=e336]: zwf
                      - text: (glucose 6-phosphate dehydrogenase,
                      - strong [ref=e337]: G6PDH
                      - text: ) and
                      - emphasis [ref=e338]: gnd
                      - text: (6-phosphogluconate dehydrogenase,
                      - strong [ref=e339]: 6PGD
                      - text: ) both encoding NADPH forming enzymes (
                      - link "Lim et al., 2002" [ref=e340] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B126"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Poulsen et al., 2005" [ref=e341] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B167"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Zhang et al., 2016a" [ref=e342] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B231"
                      - text: ); the glycolytic genes
                      - emphasis [ref=e343]: pgi
                      - text: (glucose-6-phosphate isomerase,
                      - strong [ref=e344]: GPI
                      - text: ),
                      - emphasis [ref=e345]: pfkA
                      - text: /B (phosphofructokinase I/II,
                      - strong [ref=e346]: PFK
                      - text: ), and
                      - emphasis [ref=e347]: gap
                      - text: A (G3P dehydrogenase,
                      - strong [ref=e348]: GAPDH
                      - text: ); the TCA cycle genes
                      - emphasis [ref=e349]: maeA/B
                      - text: (malic enzyme
                      - emphasis [ref=e350]: ","
                      - strong [ref=e351]: ME
                      - text: ) and
                      - emphasis [ref=e352]: icd
                      - text: (Isocitrate dehydrogenase,
                      - strong [ref=e353]: IDH
                      - text: ). The symbol (
                      - superscript [ref=e354]:
                        - strong [ref=e355]: "*"
                      - text: ) denotes enzymes that can be exchanged with functionally equivalent isozymes but with different cofactor preference (i.e., NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e356]: +
                      - text: "-dependent variants of aldehyde dehydrogenase and GAPDH). The symbol ("
                      - superscript [ref=e357]:
                        - strong [ref=e358]: "**"
                      - text: ) denotes reactions that have been either activated or deactivated depending on the envisaged redox related metabolic engineering target, as in the case of isocitrate lyase and succinyl-coA synthetase. With activated glyoxylate shunt, more malate is formed which in turn can be converted to pyruvate by the ME involving NADPH formation. In order to promote α-KG dependent redox biotransformations, the glyoxylate shunt has been deactivated forcing metabolic flux through the introduced enzyme (e.g., proline or isoleucine dioxygenases). Succinyl-CoA synthetase activity significantly promotes NADPH allocation (
                      - emphasis [ref=e359]: via
                      - text: IDH) for NADPH-dependent syntheses (e.g., xylitol production). On the other hand, deletion or reduction of this activity promotes α-KG-dependent redox biotransformations.
                  - paragraph [ref=e360]:
                    - link "Berríos-Rivera et al. (2002)" [ref=e361] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B21"
                    - text: manipulated the NADH availability in
                    - emphasis [ref=e362]: E. coli
                    - text: by regenerating NADH through the heterologous expression of
                    - emphasis [ref=e363]: Candida boidinii
                    - text: FDH regenerating one mole of NADH per mole of formate converted to CO
                    - subscript [ref=e364]: "2"
                    - text: . Natively, this FDH converts formate to CO
                    - subscript [ref=e365]: "2"
                    - text: and H
                    - subscript [ref=e366]: "2"
                    - text: without cofactor involvement. Under anaerobic conditions, the NADH availability increased from 2 to 4 mol NADH per mol glucose consumed and promoted ethanol formation. Under aerobic conditions, when formate was not formed by metabolism but added to the medium, the increased NADH availability stimulated usually inactive fermentation pathways, i.e., ethanol, lactate, and succinate formation (
                    - link "Berríos-Rivera et al., 2002" [ref=e367] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B21"
                    - text: ).
                    - link "Wang et al. (2013)" [ref=e368] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B213"
                    - text: introduced either GDH from
                    - emphasis [ref=e369]: B. subtilis
                    - text: or FDH from
                    - emphasis [ref=e370]: C. boidinii
                    - text: into a 2,3-butanediol (BD) dehydrogenase bearing
                    - emphasis [ref=e371]: E. coli
                    - text: for the production of (2S, 3S)-2,3-BD from diacetyl, i.e., a liquid fuel and a building block in asymmetric synthesis. The engineering not only improved the efficiency of (2S, 3S)-2,3-BD production, but also simplified product isolation by driving the reaction to completion and preventing the accumulation of gluconic, acetic, and lactic acid that are by-produced when only glucose is used for internal cofactor regeneration. Introduction of FDH resulted in higher (2S, 3S)-2,3-BD concentration, productivity, and yield, coming along with a large increase in the intracellular NADH concentration. However, GDH introduction poorly improved whole-cell biocatalysis efficiency, probably due to poor glucose permeation over cellular membranes and intracellular gluconic acid accumulation.
                    - emphasis [ref=e372]: E. coli
                    - text: essentially takes up glucose
                    - emphasis [ref=e373]: via
                    - text: active transport, i.e., the phosphotransferase system (PTS). Thereby, glucose is directly phosphorylated and does not become available for GDH. In crude extracts, GDH showed high activity, whereas the slow glucose permeation into cells limited GDH-mediated NADH regeneration (
                    - link "Wang et al., 2013" [ref=e374] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B213"
                    - text: ). Schewe et al. showed that the introduction of the glucose facilitator from
                    - emphasis [ref=e375]: Zymomonas mobilis
                    - text: ", mediating energy-independent glucose diffusion into cells, can overcome this limitation in CYP450 bearing"
                    - emphasis [ref=e376]: E. coli
                    - text: for the NADPH-dependent oxyfunctionalization of α-pinene to α-pinene oxide, verbenol, and myrtenol (
                    - link "Schewe et al., 2008" [ref=e377] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B174"
                    - text: ). FDH from
                    - emphasis [ref=e378]: Mycobacterium vaccae
                    - text: was applied for efficient NADH supply in
                    - emphasis [ref=e379]: E. coli
                    - text: for D-fructose reduction of to D-mannitol by coexpressed mannitol dehydrogenase from
                    - emphasis [ref=e380]: Leuconostoc pseudomesenteroides
                    - text: (
                    - link "Kaup et al., 2004" [ref=e381] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B89"
                    - text: )
                    - emphasis [ref=e382]: .
                    - text: The additional introduction of the
                    - emphasis [ref=e383]: Z. mobilis
                    - text: glucose facilitator thereby improved D-fructose uptake, enabling 84% yield. Whereas GDH from
                    - emphasis [ref=e384]: B. subtilis
                    - text: has been extensively used for
                    - emphasis [ref=e385]: in vivo
                    - text: NAD(P)H regeneration, mainly because of its dual cofactor specificity and ease of expression in
                    - emphasis [ref=e386]: E
                    - text: .
                    - emphasis [ref=e387]: coli
                    - text: ", interesting biochemical properties (e.g., activity and stability) and cofactor preference have been reported for GDHs from different organisms ("
                    - link "Pongtharangkul et al., 2015" [ref=e388] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B161"
                    - text: ).
                    - link "Pongtharangkul et al. (2015)" [ref=e389] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B161"
                    - text: cloned and expressed in both
                    - emphasis [ref=e390]: E. coli
                    - text: and
                    - emphasis [ref=e391]: B. subtilis
                    - text: a GDH gene from
                    - emphasis [ref=e392]: B. amyloliquefaciens
                    - text: (GDH-BA) and evaluated the kinetics and biochemical properties of the purified enzyme. They suggested that the high specific activity as well as the low
                    - emphasis [ref=e393]: K
                    - subscript [ref=e394]: M
                    - text: "-value towards glucose and NADP"
                    - superscript [ref=e395]: +
                    - text: make this GDH a promising candidate for
                    - emphasis [ref=e396]: in vivo
                    - text: applications. Recombinant
                    - emphasis [ref=e397]: B. subtilis
                    - text: harboring GDH-BA was successfully used to regenerate NADPH for n-hexane hydroxylation catalyzed by
                    - emphasis [ref=e398]: B. subtilis
                    - text: containing the CYP450 BM3 variant F87V (
                    - link "Pongtharangkul et al., 2015" [ref=e399] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B161"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e400]:
                    - text: By expressing
                    - emphasis [ref=e401]: adh
                    - text: from
                    - emphasis [ref=e402]: Lactobacillus brevis
                    - text: in
                    - emphasis [ref=e403]: E. coli
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Ernst et al. (2005)" [ref=e404] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B47"
                    - text: developed whole-cell biotransformation systems for the reduction of prochiral carbonyl compounds to chiral hydroxy acid derivatives. The
                    - emphasis [ref=e405]: fdh
                    - text: gene from
                    - emphasis [ref=e406]: M. vaccae
                    - text: N10 thereby was coexpressed for NADH regeneration and, in the presence of formate, increased the intracellular NADH/NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e407]: +
                    - text: ratio 7-fold. This enabled the formation of 40 mM methyl (R)-3-hydroxy butanoate from methyl acetoacetate with 100% yield. Also
                    - emphasis [ref=e408]: gdh
                    - text: coexpression together with genes encoding carbonyl reductases in
                    - emphasis [ref=e409]: E. coli
                    - text: has been shown to be valuable for the production of chiral alcohols (
                    - link "Kataoka et al., 2003" [ref=e410] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B86"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e411]:
                    - text: For the production of chemicals from CO
                    - subscript [ref=e412]: "2"
                    - text: ", also the photoautotrophic redox metabolism gains increasing attention. However, the cofactor bias of phototrophs, with NADPH as main source of reduction equivalents, differs from that of heterotrophs. For D-lactate production, Li et al. changed the cofactor preference of the key enzyme D-lactate dehydrogenase (LdhD) from NADH to NADPH. The engineered LdhD in"
                    - emphasis [ref=e413]: Synechococcus elongatus
                    - text: PCC 7942 then enabled efficient light-driven production of optically pure D-lactate from CO
                    - subscript [ref=e414]: "2"
                    - text: . Mutation of LdhD increased D-lactate productivity by over 3.6-fold. The additional introduction of a lactic acid transporter and bubbling CO
                    - subscript [ref=e415]: "2"
                    - text: "-enriched air further enhanced D-lactate concentration and productivity ("
                    - link "Li et al., 2015a" [ref=e416] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B115"
                    - text: ). For phototrophic D-lactate synthesis from CO
                    - subscript [ref=e417]: "2"
                    - text: by
                    - emphasis [ref=e418]: Synechocystis
                    - text: sp. PCC 6803,
                    - link "Varman et al. (2013)" [ref=e419] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B204"
                    - text: utilized a mutated GDH. Together with codon optimization and heterologous expression of a soluble transhydrogenase, this approach significantly improved D-lactate synthesis. Whereas
                    - emphasis [ref=e420]: in vivo
                    - text: NAD(P)H regeneration systems have been widely studied, NAD(P)
                    - superscript [ref=e421]: +
                    - text: regeneration has been less explored and can be considered less demanding. Using the H
                    - subscript [ref=e422]: "2"
                    - text: O producing NADH oxidase from
                    - emphasis [ref=e423]: L. brevis
                    - text: as heterologous NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e424]: +
                    - text: regenerating enzyme together with a NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e425]: +
                    - text: "-dependent (2R, 3R)-2,3-BD dehydrogenase from"
                    - emphasis [ref=e426]: B. subtilis
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Xiao et al. (2010)" [ref=e427] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B221"
                    - text: developed an
                    - emphasis [ref=e428]: E. coli
                    - text: biocatalyst for the production of (3S)-acetoin from meso-2,3-BD, (3R)-acetoin from (2R, 3R)-2,3-BD, and (2S, 3S)-2,3-BD from a substrate mixture. Their work demonstrated that coupling NADH oxidase from
                    - emphasis [ref=e429]: L. brevis
                    - text: with NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e430]: +
                    - text: "-dependent dehydrogenases provides an efficient platform for NAD"
                    - superscript [ref=e431]: +
                    - text: "-dependent whole-cell biotransformations."
              - generic [ref=e432]:
                - heading "3 Pathway Engineering" [level=2] [ref=e433]: 3 Pathway Engineering
                - paragraph [ref=e434]:
                  - text: Central carbon metabolism generally refers to the main NAD(P)H-generating catabolic pathways. Whereas NADH especially fuels aerobic energy metabolism, NADPH is consumed especially in anabolic pathways. In photoautotrophs, NADPH additionally functions as provider of reduction equivalents for CO
                  - subscript [ref=e435]: "2"
                  - text: fixation and thus constitutes the main carrier of reduction equivalents under light conditions. Heterotrophs rely on catabolism for NAD(P)H regeneration with a bias towards NADH. Catabolic modules have been widely exploited to fuel redox-intensive production processes. Key enzymes involved in catabolic NAD(P)H regeneration are summarized in
                  - link "Table 1" [ref=e436] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#T1"
                  - text: ", which gives respective reactions, cofactor specificities, and pathway affiliations. In the following"
                  - link "Sections 3.1" [ref=e437] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#s2-1"
                  - text: and
                  - link "3.2" [ref=e438] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#s2-2"
                  - text: ", approaches followed for the engineering of these pathways for NADPH and NADH regeneration are summarized and discussed. Further, the main approaches for heterotrophic metabolism are visualized in"
                  - link "Figure 2" [ref=e439] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#F2"
                  - text: .
                  - link "Section 3.3" [ref=e440] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#s2-3"
                  - text: then focusses on the engineering of phototrophic metabolic modules for whole-cell redox biocatalysis. At this point, the value of NAD(P)H biosensors as useful tools to identify genetic manipulations leading to improved NADPH availability is highlighted (
                  - link "Zhang et al., 2016a" [ref=e441] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#B231"
                  - text: ).
                - generic [ref=e442]:
                  - heading "TABLE 1." [level=3] [ref=e443]

                        - cell "D-glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate + NADP+ + H2O → 3-phospho-D-glycerate + NADPH + 2 H+" [ref=e477]:

                          - superscript [ref=e485]: +

                      - 'row "NAD+-dependent glycerol-3-phosphate dehydrogenase (GPDH; EC: 1.1.1.8) Glycerate 3-phosphate + NAD+ → Phosphoenolpyruvate + NADH EMPP" [ref=e490]':
                        - 'cell "NAD+-dependent glycerol-3-phosphate dehydrogenase (GPDH; EC: 1.1.1.8)" [ref=e491]':

                        - cell "Glycerate 3-phosphate + NAD+ → Phosphoenolpyruvate + NADH" [ref=e493]:

                      - row "NAD(P)+-dependent isocitrate dehydrogenase (IDH; EC:1.1.1.41, 1.1.1.42) Isocitrate + NAD(P)+ → α-ketoglutarate + CO2 + NAD(P)H + H+ TCA cycle" [ref=e496]:
                        - cell "NAD(P)+-dependent isocitrate dehydrogenase (IDH; EC:1.1.1.41, 1.1.1.42)" [ref=e497]:

                        - 'cell "α-Ketoglutarate dehydrogenase complex (αKGDH; EC: 1.2.4.2, 2.3.1.6, 1.8.1.4)" [ref=e514]'

                        - 'cell "Pyruvate dehydrogenase complex (PDC; EC: 1.2.4.1)" [ref=e520]'

                      - 'row "NAD+-D-xylose dehydrogenase (XDH; EC: 1.1.1.175) D-xylose + NAD+ → D-xylonolactone + NADH + H+ Dahms pathway" [ref=e525]':
                        - 'cell "NAD+-D-xylose dehydrogenase (XDH; EC: 1.1.1.175)" [ref=e526]':
                          - text: NAD
                          - superscript [ref=e527]: +
                          - text: "-D-xylose dehydrogenase (XDH; EC: 1.1.1.175)"
                        - cell "D-xylose + NAD+ → D-xylonolactone + NADH + H+" [ref=e528]:
                          - text: D-xylose + NAD
                          - superscript [ref=e529]: +
