                          - superscript [ref=e530]: +
                        - cell "Dahms pathway" [ref=e531]
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e533] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: table/T1/
                - generic [ref=e534]:
                  - heading "3.1 Engineering the Glycolytic and the Pentose Phosphate Pathways" [level=3] [ref=e535]
                  - generic [ref=e536]:
                    - heading "3.1.1 Overexpression of PPP Genes" [level=4] [ref=e537]
                    - paragraph [ref=e538]:
                      - text: Metabolic engineering to increase the NADPH yield per glucose consumed focused on the overexpression of the PPP genes
                      - emphasis [ref=e539]: zwf
                      - text: (glucose 6-phosphate dehydrogenase, G6PDH, EC 1.1.1.49) and
                      - emphasis [ref=e540]: gnd
                      - text: (6-phosphogluconate dehydrogenase, 6PGD, EC 1.1.1.44) both encoding NADPH forming enzymes (
                      - link "Lim et al., 2002" [ref=e541] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B126"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Poulsen et al., 2005" [ref=e542] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B167"
                      - text: ) and/or the deletion of the glycolytic genes
                      - emphasis [ref=e543]: pgi
                      - text: (glucose-6-phosphate isomerase, GPI, EC 5.3.1.9) and
                      - emphasis [ref=e544]: pfkA/B
                      - text: (phosphofructokinase I/II, PFK, EC 2.7.1.11) (
                      - link "Canonaco et al., 2001" [ref=e545] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B33"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Lee et al., 2010" [ref=e546] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B109"
                      - text: ) (
                      - link "Figure 2" [ref=e547] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#F2"
                      - text: ).
                      - link "Fuhrer et al. (2005)" [ref=e548] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B55"
                      - text: estimated that in growing
                      - emphasis [ref=e549]: E. coli
                      - text: cells, 12% of the glucose is catabolized
                      - emphasis [ref=e550]: via
                      - text: the PPP. The deletion of
                      - emphasis [ref=e551]: pgi
                      - text: prohibits glucose catabolism
                      - emphasis [ref=e552]: via
                      - text: glycolysis and enforces catabolism via the PPP, with 2 mol NADPH mol
                      - superscript [ref=e553]: −1
                      - text: glucose in a linear, non-cyclic fashion, plus the NADPH regenerated in the TCA cycle. Complete oxidation of glucose
                      - emphasis [ref=e554]: via
                      - text: a cyclic PPP requires complete cycling of both fructose 6-phosphate and triose phosphate and theoretically yields 12 mol NADPH per mole glucose (
                      - link "Kruger and von Schaewen, 2003" [ref=e555] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B100"
                      - text: ). The ∆
                      - emphasis [ref=e556]: pgi
                      - text: mutant shows slower glucose uptake and a growth defect probably due to limited PPP flux capacity or regulatory effects on enzymes involved in glucose uptake or the PPP (
                      - link "Fuhrer et al., 2005" [ref=e557] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B55"
                      - text: ). While deletion of
                      - emphasis [ref=e558]: pgi
                      - text: renders cyclization impossible, deletions in
                      - emphasis [ref=e559]: pfkAB
                      - text: redirect fructose 6-phosphate into the PPP further promoting catabolism
                      - emphasis [ref=e560]: via
                      - text: the PPP (
                      - link "Siedler et al., 2011" [ref=e561] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B185"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e562]:
                      - text: Overexpression of
                      - emphasis [ref=e563]: zwf
                      - text: and
                      - emphasis [ref=e564]: gnd
                      - text: also was found to enhance NADPH-dependent PHB synthesis by recombinant
                      - emphasis [ref=e565]: E. coli
                      - text: ", with"
                      - emphasis [ref=e566]: zwf
                      - text: overexpression being three times more effective than
                      - emphasis [ref=e567]: gnd
                      - text: overexpression for increasing the NADPH (
                      - link "Lim et al., 2002" [ref=e568] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B126"
                      - text: ).
                      - link "Lee et al. (2007)" [ref=e569] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B113"
                      - text: produced ɛ-caprolactone from cyclohexanone using whole cells of recombinant
                      - emphasis [ref=e570]: E. coli
                      - text: expressing cyclohexanone monooxygenase of
                      - emphasis [ref=e571]: Acinetobacter calcoaceticus
                      - text: . The maximum ɛ-caprolactone concentration was achieved by coexpression of the
                      - emphasis [ref=e572]: zwf
                      - text: gene enabling a 39% enhancement compared with the control. For (R)-3-hydroxybutyrate (3HB) production,
                      - link "Perez-Zabaleta et al. (2016)" [ref=e573] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B158"
                      - text: introduced the 3HB pathway of
                      - emphasis [ref=e574]: Halomonas boliviensis
                      - text: into
                      - emphasis [ref=e575]: E. coli
                      - text: ", involving acetoacetyl-CoA reductase, which accepts both NADH and NADPH. The V"
                      - subscript [ref=e576]: max
                      - text: for the latter is however eight times higher so that the less abundant NADPH limits production. Overexpression of
                      - emphasis [ref=e577]: zwf
                      - text: together with nitrogen depletion gave the highest yield and a 50% increase in 3HB concentration. Overexpression of PPP genes also has successfully been applied in organisms other than
                      - emphasis [ref=e578]: E. coli
                      - text: . For instance, the production of the poly-γ-glutamic acid (γ-PGA), a multifunctional non-toxic, biodegradable biopolymer, in
                      - emphasis [ref=e579]: Bacillus licheniformis
                      - text: was improved by 35% when
                      - emphasis [ref=e580]: zwf
                      - text: was overexpressed (
                      - link "Cai et al., 2017" [ref=e581] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B31"
                      - text: ). In
                      - emphasis [ref=e582]: Aspergillus niger
                      - text: ", 13-fold 6PGD overproduction increased the intracellular NADPH concentration 2- to 9-fold ("
                      - link "Poulsen et al., 2005" [ref=e583] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B167"
                      - text: ). It should however be noted that the overexpression of PPP genes may compromise target product formation if the substrate is used for growth, cofactor regeneration, as well as product formation as one carbon atom is lost
                      - emphasis [ref=e584]: via
                      - text: decarboxylation (6PGD-catalyzed reaction). In the case of NADPH-dependent GDP-L-fucose biosynthesis from glucose, for instance,
                      - emphasis [ref=e585]: zwf
                      - text: overexpression decreased the production under glucose-limiting condition even though the intracellular NADPH level was increased compared to the control strain. Fine-tuning of the glucose feeding strategy improved the GDP-L-fucose production by 21% with
                      - emphasis [ref=e586]: zwf
                      - text: overexpression, highlighting that sufficient supplementation of the carbon and energy source is critical when the PPP is engineered for NADPH-dependent biotransformations (
                      - link "Lee et al., 2011" [ref=e587] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B111"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Lee et al., 2013" [ref=e588] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B112"
                      - text: ).
                  - generic [ref=e589]:
                    - heading "3.1.2 Redirection of Glycolytic Fluxes to the PPP" [level=4] [ref=e590]
                    - paragraph [ref=e591]:
                      - text: Apart from overexpressing
                      - emphasis [ref=e592]: zwf
                      - text: and
                      - emphasis [ref=e593]: gnd
                      - text: ", deletion of"
                      - emphasis [ref=e594]: pgi
                      - text: ","
                      - emphasis [ref=e595]: pfkA
                      - text: ", and/or"
                      - emphasis [ref=e596]: pfkB
                      - text: can also redirect the metabolic flux from glycolysis into the PPP (
                      - link "Figure 2" [ref=e597] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#F2"
                      - text: ) and has been employed in the NADPH-dependent production of various chemicals (e.g., hydrogen, amino acids, lycopene, 2-chloropropionic acid, terpenoids) (
                      - link "Aslan et al., 2017" [ref=e598] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B9"
                      - text: ).
                      - link "Canonaco et al. (2001)" [ref=e599] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B33"
                      - text: showed via
                      - superscript [ref=e600]: "13"
                      - text: C-MFA that glucose catabolism in
                      - emphasis [ref=e601]: E. coli
                      - text: Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e602]: pgi
                      - text: proceeds predominantly (almost 100%)
                      - emphasis [ref=e603]: via
                      - text: the PPP. Using a theoretical approach, the NADPH production rate was proposed to increase by 300% upon
                      - emphasis [ref=e604]: pgi
                      - text: deletion (
                      - link "Chemler et al., 2010" [ref=e605] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B38"
                      - text: ). The complete flux through the PPP in the Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e606]: pgi
                      - text: mutant was also independent of the growth phase in batch cultivation (
                      - link "Toya et al., 2010" [ref=e607] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B198"
                      - text: ). In thymidine production, an important precursor of various antiviral drugs, NADPH is used not only for the conversion of uridine diphosphate to deoxyuridine monophosphate, but also for the recycling of tetrahydrofolate to dihydrofolate, a co-substrate for thymidine biosynthesis. Similarly, to
                      - emphasis [ref=e608]: zwf
                      - text: overexpression,
                      - link "Lee et al. (2010)" [ref=e609] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B109"
                      - text: reported that
                      - emphasis [ref=e610]: pgi
                      - text: deletion resulted in a 39% higher NADPH/NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e611]: +
                      - text: ratio which led to a 5-fold enhancement in thymidine yield on glucose. In the same manner, the NADPH-dependent production οf leucocyanidin and catechin from dihydroquercetin increased upon
                      - emphasis [ref=e612]: pgi
                      - text: deletion in
                      - emphasis [ref=e613]: E. coli
                      - text: (
                      - link "Chemler et al., 2010" [ref=e614] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B38"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e615]:
                      - text: Deletions in
                      - emphasis [ref=e616]: pfkAB
                      - text: theoretically can afford even higher NADPH yields on glucose by recycling fructose 6-phosphate to glucose 6-phosphate (
                      - link "Kruger and von Schaewen, 2003" [ref=e617] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B100"
                      - text: ). This partial cycling involves catabolism of PPP-derived glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate (G3P)
                      - emphasis [ref=e618]: via
                      - text: the lower part of glycolysis (EMPP) and the TCA cycle. Under resting cell conditions, this flux through EMPP and TCA cycle is necessary and beneficial for energy generation and PTS-driven glucose uptake (
                      - link "Siedler et al., 2011" [ref=e619] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B185"
                      - text: ). For the production of methyl 3-hydroxybutyrate (MHB), a building block for widely used cholesterol-lowering statins (
                      - link "Poulsen et al., 2005" [ref=e620] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B167"
                      - text: ), the prochiral β-ketoester methyl acetoacetate was reduced in recombinant
                      - emphasis [ref=e621]: E. coli
                      - text: by the NADPH-dependent R-specific alcohol dehydrogenase (ADH) from
                      - emphasis [ref=e622]: L. brevis
                      - text: . The MHB yield on glucose was almost doubled
                      - emphasis [ref=e623]: via pfk
                      - text: A deletion, which was attributed to partial PPP cyclization, which was confirmed by
                      - superscript [ref=e624]: "13"
                      - text: C-MFA revealing a negative net flux
                      - emphasis [ref=e625]: via
                      - text: PGI (
                      - link "Siedler et al., 2012" [ref=e626] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B184"
                      - text: ). For further process optimization,
                      - link "Siedler et al. (2012)" [ref=e627] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B184"
                      - text: overexpressed the glucose facilitator and glucokinase genes of
                      - emphasis [ref=e628]: Z. mobilis
                      - text: and deleted either
                      - emphasis [ref=e629]: pgi
                      - text: ","
                      - emphasis [ref=e630]: pfk
                      - text: A, or
                      - emphasis [ref=e631]: pfk
                      - text: A plus
                      - emphasis [ref=e632]: pfk
                      - text: B. In all cases, the glucose uptake rate increased (30–47%), and, for the Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e633]: pgi
                      - text: and Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e634]: pfk
                      - text: A strains, the specific MHB production rate increased by 15 and 20%, respectively.
                      - link "Siedler et al. (2013)" [ref=e635] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B186"
                      - text: also investigated
                      - emphasis [ref=e636]: pfk
                      - text: A or
                      - emphasis [ref=e637]: gap
                      - text: A (encoding G3P dehydrogenase) deletion in ADH carrying
                      - emphasis [ref=e638]: C. glutamicum
                      - text: . The WT showed a specific MHB production rate of 3.1 mmol g
                      - subscript [ref=e639]: CDW
                      - superscript [ref=e640]: −1
                      - text: h
                      - superscript [ref=e641]: −1
                      - text: and a MHB yield of 2.7 mol per mol glucose, whereas the ∆
                      - emphasis [ref=e642]: pfkA
                      - text: mutant showed a similar rate, but a yield of 4.8 mol mol
                      - superscript [ref=e643]: −1
                      - text: . The specific activity of the Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e644]: gapA
                      - text: mutant decreased by 62% compared to the other strains, but the MHB yield increased to 7.9 mol per mol glucose.
                    - paragraph [ref=e645]:
                      - text: Likewise, the PPP of
                      - emphasis [ref=e646]: Streptomyces
                      - text: ", a genus widely used for the synthesis of various antibiotics, was successfully modulated."
                      - link "Borodina et al. (2008)" [ref=e647] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B25"
                      - text: deleted the
                      - emphasis [ref=e648]: pfkA2
                      - text: gene, one of the three annotated
                      - emphasis [ref=e649]: pfkA
                      - text: homologues in
                      - emphasis [ref=e650]: S. coelicolor
                      - text: A3, for the overproduction of the pigmented antibiotics actinorhodin and undecylprodigiosin. The
                      - emphasis [ref=e651]: pfkA2
                      - text: deletion increased the carbon flux through the PPP, as measured by
                      - superscript [ref=e652]: "13"
                      - text: C-MFA, involving increased intracellular glucose 6-phosphate and fructose 6-phosphate levels. Similarly, PPP flux was enhanced by deleting
                      - emphasis [ref=e653]: gap
                      - text: 1 and
                      - emphasis [ref=e654]: gap
                      - text: "2"
                      - emphasis [ref=e655]: in S. clavuligerus
                      - text: ", thereby increasing the supply of the precursor G3P for the production of clavulanic acid, a β-lactamase inhibitor ("
                      - link "Li and Townsend, 2006" [ref=e656] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B117"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e657]:
                      - text: It is well reported that the NADPH supply, e.g.,
                      - emphasis [ref=e658]: via
                      - text: the PPP, also is crucial for lipid and isoprenoid production by yeast (
                      - link "Zu et al., 2020" [ref=e659] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B239"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Lazar et al., 2018" [ref=e660] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B108"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Qiao et al., 2017" [ref=e661] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B163"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Wasylenko et al., 2015" [ref=e662] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B215"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Runguphan and Keasling, 2014" [ref=e663] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B168"
                      - text: ). Apart from diverting carbon flux from glycolysis to the PPP through genetic manipulations (
                      - link "Figure 2" [ref=e664] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#F2"
                      - text: ), medium engineering (i.e., synergistic cofeeding) is also reported to be effective.
                      - link "Park et al. (2019)" [ref=e665] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B155"
                      - text: report that the coordination of carbons, ATP, and reduction equivalents required for lipid synthesis can be optimized by feeding substrate mixtures fueling diverse pathways. Controlled cofeeding of superior ATP and NADPH generating substrates, i.e., glucose to support H
                      - subscript [ref=e666]: "2"
                      - text: "-driven CO"
                      - subscript [ref=e667]: "2"
                      - text: reduction and gluconate to drive acetate reduction, enabled not only circumvention of catabolite repression, but also synergistic co-metabolism in two divergent organisms, i.e.,
                      - emphasis [ref=e668]: Moorella thermoacetica
                      - text: and
                      - emphasis [ref=e669]: Yarrowia lipolytica
                      - text: . Glucose doping of
                      - emphasis [ref=e670]: M. thermoacetica
                      - text: stimulated CO
                      - subscript [ref=e671]: "2"
                      - text: reduction into acetate by augmenting ATP synthesis
                      - emphasis [ref=e672]: via
                      - text: pyruvate kinase (PEP + ADP → pyruvate + ATP), and gluconate doping of
                      - emphasis [ref=e673]: Y. lipolytica
                      - text: accelerated acetate-driven lipogenesis by NADPH synthesis through cyclic PPP. Together, synergistic cofeeding was calculated to produce CO
                      - subscript [ref=e674]: "2"
                      - text: "-derived lipids with up to 38% energy yield (referred to H"
                      - subscript [ref=e675]: "2"
                      - text: energy). Their stoichiometric analysis suggested that glucose and gluconate complemented ATP and NADPH generation, alleviating limitations seen in acetogenesis and lipogenesis, respectively. Tracing
                      - superscript [ref=e676]: "13"
                      - text: C-labelled glucose and gluconate revealed that cells used these substrates almost exclusively for ATP and NADPH production locally within glycolysis and the PPP, respectively. In particular, PPP activation by gluconate cofeeding solved the challenge of NADPH limitation in acetate-fed cells.
                    - paragraph [ref=e677]:
                      - text: It should be noted that strategies involving a PPP knockout in combination with attenuated glycolysis can also be beneficial for biosynthetic processes, e.g., hyaluronic acid production. Based on a genome-scale metabolic model and respective simulation, Cheng et al., constructed a superior
                      - emphasis [ref=e678]: C. glutamicum
                      - text: strain for high-titer hyaluronic acid biosynthesis (
                      - link "Cheng et al., 2019" [ref=e679] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B40"
                      - text: ). Genetic modulations included enhancement of the hyaluronic acid biosynthesis pathway,
                      - emphasis [ref=e680]: zwf
                      - text: deletion, fructose bisphosphate aldolase (FBA) attenuation, lactate/acetate pathway knockout, and pyruvate dehydrogenase complex (PDH) attenuation to drive carbon flux from the precursor metabolites glucose-6-phosphate (G6P), fructose-6-phosphate (F6P), and byproducts (lactate, acetate, succinate) to hyaluronic acid biosynthesis. The engineered
                      - emphasis [ref=e681]: C. glutamicum
                      - text: strain produced 28.7 g L
                      - superscript [ref=e682]: −1
                      - text: HA in a fed-batch culture, while the byproduct concentration was reduced by half.
                  - generic [ref=e683]:
                    - heading "3.1.3 Switching the Cofactor Preference of Glycolytic Enzymes" [level=4] [ref=e684]
                    - paragraph [ref=e685]:
                      - text: Since the first report on redesigning the coenzyme specificity of a dehydrogenase by directed mutagenesis and molecular modelling (
                      - link "Scrutton et al., 1990" [ref=e686] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B179"
                      - text: ), switching cofactor preference has been a target not only for enzymatic biocatalysis but also for engineering cellular metabolism towards increased product yields. The case of ethanol production from pentoses by
                      - emphasis [ref=e687]: Saccharomyces cerevisiae
                      - text: is one of the most studied examples of how switching the cofactor dependency of the involved metabolic pathways can enhance product yield and decrease unwanted side products such as xylitol and CO
                      - subscript [ref=e688]: "2"
                      - text: (
                      - link "Jeppsson et al., 2002" [ref=e689] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B77"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Verho et al., 2003" [ref=e690] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B207"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Petschacher and Nidetzky, 2008" [ref=e691] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B159"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Bengtsson et al., 2009" [ref=e692] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B16"
                      - text: ). Apart from microbial platforms for ethanol or other fermentation product formation, switching cofactor preference of glycolytic enzymes also was applied for the bioproduction of industrially relevant terpenoids. These secondary metabolites are synthesized in dedicated biosynthetic pathways linked to the primary metabolism by specific precursors and various cofactors.
                      - link "Kim et al. (2018)" [ref=e693] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B93"
                      - text: engineered
                      - emphasis [ref=e694]: S. cerevisiae
                      - text: for the production of protopanaxadiol, a type of aglycone ginsenoside, by introducing the respective biosynthetic pathway and rerouting the redox metabolism to improve NADPH availability. By replacing the cytoplasmic NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e695]: +
                      - text: "-utilizing aldehyde dehydrogenase converting acetaldehyde to acetate with a functionally equivalent NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e696]: +
                      - text: "-dependent isozyme ("
                      - link "Figure 2" [ref=e697] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#F2"
                      - text: ), the final protopanaxadiol titer could be increased more than 11-fold (
                      - link "Kim et al., 2018" [ref=e698] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B93"
                      - text: ).
                      - link "Kildegaard et al. (2016)" [ref=e699] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B92"
                      - text: engineered
                      - emphasis [ref=e700]: S. cerevisiae
                      - text: for high-level 3-hydroxypropionic acid (3HP) production by increasing biosynthetic gene copy numbers and improving flux towards precursors and redox cofactors. 3HP was produced
                      - emphasis [ref=e701]: via
                      - text: a malonyl-CoA reductase (MCR) that reduces malonyl-CoA to 3HP at the expense of two NADPH. Genomic integration of multiple copies of
                      - emphasis [ref=e702]: Chloroflexus aurantiacus
                      - text: MCR and phosphorylation-deficient acetyl-CoA carboxylase genes increased the 3HP titer 5-fold in comparison with single integration. Furthermore, the native NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e703]: +
                      - text: "-dependent glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate dehydrogenase (GAPDH) was exchanged with a heterologous NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e704]: +
                      - text: "-dependent version from"
                      - emphasis [ref=e705]: Chlostridium acetobutylicum
                      - text: to increase NADPH formation (
                      - link "Figure 2" [ref=e706] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#F2"
                      - text: ) and thus improve 3HP production. However, even though the two major pathways for NADPH regeneration (i.e., PPP and TCA cycle) were upregulated and carried higher flux in the engineered strain, both NADPH/NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e707]: +
                      - text: as well as NADH/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e708]: +
                      - text: ratios increased, underlining the complexity of redox metabolism (
                      - link "Kildegaard et al., 2016" [ref=e709] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B92"
                      - text: ). The same GAPDH exchange was reported to enhance lycopene and ε-caprolactone production in
                      - emphasis [ref=e710]: E. coli
                      - text: (
                      - link "Martínez et al., 2008" [ref=e711] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B138"
                      - text: ).
                  - generic [ref=e712]:
                    - heading "3.1.4 Engineering the Entner–Doudoroff Pathway" [level=4] [ref=e713]
                    - paragraph [ref=e714]:
                      - text: Microorganisms utilize three major pathways to break down glucose into pyruvate, i.e., EMPP, EDP, and oxidative PPP (OPPP) (
                      - link "Figure 2" [ref=e715] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#F2"
                      - text: ).
                      - emphasis [ref=e716]: E. coli
                      - text: mainly relies on the EMPP and the OPPP, while the EDP is mainly inactive except during growth on gluconate. Interestingly, the EDP was found to be active in the cyanobacterium
                      - emphasis [ref=e717]: Synechocystis
                      - text: sp. PCC 6803, whose nutrient- rather than ATP-limited lifestyle profits from the lower costs for enzyme synthesis (
                      - link "Chen et al., 2016" [ref=e718] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B39"
                      - text: ). In these phototrophs, the EDP seems to be important for the internal glycogen breakdown under fluctuating carbon availability (
                      - link "Lucius et al., 2021" [ref=e719] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B134"
                      - text: ). Physiological analyses of different glycolytic strategies revealed that the EDP could be a preferred pathway based on both thermodynamics and the considerably lower enzyme costs. The EMPP operates 10 enzymatic reactions to yield two pyruvates, two ATP, and two NADH per glucose molecule, the OPPP provides two NADPH at the expense of one carbon atom lost as CO
                      - subscript [ref=e720]: "2"
                      - text: ", and the EDP operates five enzymes to produce one pyruvate, one glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate, one ATP, one NADH and one NADPH per glucose molecule often constituting a well-balanced product mix. Further, the EDP bypasses the two thermodynamic bottlenecks of the EMPP, i.e., fructose 1,6-bisphosphate aldolase and triose-phosphate isomerase but at the expense of ATP yield. This characterizes EMPP and EDP as routes with high protein synthesis costs and poor ATP yield, respectively ("
                      - link "Hollinshead et al., 2016" [ref=e721] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B64"
                      - text: ). With an active EMPP, GAPDH is the enzyme showing the highest NADH formation rate during aerobic as well as anaerobic growth of
                      - emphasis [ref=e722]: E. coli
                      - text: . Many organisms lack this route and use the EDP instead with both GAPDH and glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase showing high rates (
                      - link "Schrewe et al., 2013" [ref=e723] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B176"
                      - text: ). EDP-derived NADPH supports oxidative stress responses and, in contrast to PPP-derived NADPH, is formed without carbon loss (i.e., CO
                      - subscript [ref=e724]: "2"
                      - text: formation) (
                      - link "Hollinshead et al., 2016" [ref=e725] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B64"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Jojima et al., 2021" [ref=e726] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B80"
                      - text: ). Recently, EDP upregulation in
                      - emphasis [ref=e727]: E. coli
                      - text: was shown to enhance glucose consumption as well as G3P and pyruvate formation serving as precursors of isoprenoid synthesis
                      - emphasis [ref=e728]: via
                      - text: the methylerythritol 4-phosphate (MEP) pathway (
                      - link "Jojima et al., 2021" [ref=e729] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B80"
                      - text: ). The latter depends on high and balanced flux among precursors, cofactors, and cellular energy and is limited by the imbalanced supply of G3P and pyruvate in
                      - emphasis [ref=e730]: E. coli
                      - text: . To address this problem,
                      - link "Li et al. (2015b)" [ref=e731] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B116"
                      - text: either knocked out or overexpressed multiple gene targets to redistribute fluxes among EMPP, EDP, and PPP and thus improve carotenoid production. Directing metabolic flux from EMPP towards EDP together with an enhanced MEP pathway improved carotenoid synthesis, which was further improved by
                      - emphasis [ref=e732]: pgi
                      - text: deletion. On the contrary,
                      - emphasis [ref=e733]: pfkAB
                      - text: deletion had a negative effect. Whereas
                      - emphasis [ref=e734]: pgi
                      - text: deletion blocks the recycling of F6P and G3P into the OPPP and supports flux towards the MEP substrates pyruvate and G3P,
                      - emphasis [ref=e735]: pfkAB
                      - text: deletion enables F6P cyclization into the PPP. Importantly, improved carotenoid yields were accompanied by increased biomass growth and decreased acetate overflow, indicating a good balance between carotenoid biosynthesis and cell metabolism (
                      - link "Li et al., 2015b" [ref=e736] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B116"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e737]:
                      - text: In a similar attempt to improve isoprenoid biosynthesis from glucose and xylose in
                      - emphasis [ref=e738]: E. coli
                      - text: ","
                      - link "Liu et al. (2013)" [ref=e739] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B130"
                      - text: evaluated EMPP, EDP, PPP, as well as the Dahms pathway as MEP feeding modules differing in their modes of G3P, pyruvate, and reducing equivalent generation. In EMPP and PPP, G3P generation precedes pyruvate formation, whereas the Dahms pathway generates pyruvate from G3P. Only the EDP simultaneously produces the two MEP precursors in one reaction. Although lower amounts of precursors are produced when glucose is metabolized via the PPP, more reducing equivalents can be generated compared to EMPP and EDP. From glucose, feeding modules containing the EDP enabled three and six times higher isoprene titers and yields, respectively, compared to the EMPP. From xylose, the PPP module was significantly more effective than the Dahms pathway. In terms of precursor generation and energy/reducing-equivalent supply from glucose, the EDP together with the PPP was found to be the optimal module combination (
                      - link "Liu et al., 2013" [ref=e740] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B130"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e741]:
                      - text: Furthermore, OPPP and EDP were investigated as engineering targets for mevalonate production. In an
                      - emphasis [ref=e742]: E. coli
                      - text: strain expressing the
                      - emphasis [ref=e743]: Enterococcus faecalis
                      - text: genes
                      - emphasis [ref=e744]: mvaE
                      - text: and
                      - emphasis [ref=e745]: mvaS
                      - text: ", one mevalonate molecule is synthesized from three acetyl-CoA molecules using two molecules of NADPH in a 3-step reaction. Nagai et al. studied thermodynamic states of the central metabolism to identify metabolic reactions with regulatory function. Based on metabolite concentration data, they calculated ΔG and substrate saturation for each metabolic reaction, comparing control and mevalonate producing strains. Their analyses showed that further activation of thermodynamically feasible reactions in the upper part of glycolysis and the PPP appeared difficult and that metabolic bypassing"
                      - emphasis [ref=e746]: via
                      - text: the EDP was a promising strategy to increase acetyl-CoA and NADPH supply. Deletion of
                      - emphasis [ref=e747]: gnd
                      - text: and
                      - emphasis [ref=e748]: gntR
                      - text: (encoding a negative regulator of the expression of two EDP genes) increased mevalonate yield and specific production rate by 113 and 158%, respectively (
                      - link "Nagai et al., 2018" [ref=e749] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B145"
                      - text: ). Redirection to the EDP has also been employed for the production of isopropyl alcohol (
                      - link "Okahashi et al., 2017" [ref=e750] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B149"
                      - text: ), poly(3-hydroxybutyrate) (
                      - link "Zhang et al., 2014" [ref=e751] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B234"
                      - text: ), and isobutanol (
                      - link "Noda et al., 2019" [ref=e752] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B148"
                      - text: ) with
                      - emphasis [ref=e753]: E. coli
                      - text: . Instead of using existing operons,
                      - link "Ng et al. (2015)" [ref=e754] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B146"
                      - text: ", rationally engineered synthetic operons for the 5-enzyme pathway of"
                      - emphasis [ref=e755]: Z. mobilis
                      - text: and increased the NADPH regeneration rate 25-fold. By combining the synthetic EDP with an optimized terpenoid pathway, the terpenoid titer was increased by 97% (
                      - link "Ng et al., 2015" [ref=e756] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B146"
                      - text: ). These successful attempts clearly show that even though microbial cells prefer EMPP for optimal growth, metabolic engineering strategies involving the EDP appear very promising to improve product formation rates and yields.
                - generic [ref=e757]:
                  - heading "3.2 Increasing the Availability of Redox Cofactors and Cosubstrates Derived From the TCA Cycle" [level=3] [ref=e758]
                  - paragraph [ref=e759]:
                    - text: The TCA cycle is the primary metabolic pathway enabling aerobic organisms to oxidize diverse organic compounds, such as carbohydrates, lipids, or amino acids, to provide redox equivalents, energy, and precursors for biomass formation to the cell. Biotransformations can profit from the TCA cycle for the provision not only of redox cofactors but also of TCA intermediates as cosubstrates, as in the case of α-ketoglutarate (α-KG) dependent dioxygenases (
                    - link "Figure 2" [ref=e760] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#F2"
                    - text: ). Here, it has to be considered that the TCA cycle of cyanobacteria is different from that of heterotrophic bacteria (
                    - link "Knoop et al., 2013" [ref=e761] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B97"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Xiong et al., 2014" [ref=e762] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B222"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Zhang et al., 2016b" [ref=e763] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B232"
                    - text: ), as it lacks α-KG dehydrogenase and thus cannot convert α-KG to succinyl–CoA. The cycle was however shown to be completed by two alternative enzymes, a novel α-KG decarboxylase and a succinic semialdehyde dehydrogenase. This corrected the misconception that these organisms have an incomplete TCA cycle and shed light on new metabolic potentials for biotechnological exploitation (
                    - link "Zhang et al., 2016b" [ref=e764] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B232"
                    - text: ). The synthesis of
                    - emphasis [ref=e765]: trans
                    - text: "-4-hydroxy-L-proline by both heterotrophic and photosynthetic cells using an α-KG dependent L-proline-4-hydroxylase is such a successful example ("
                    - link "Falcioni et al., 2015" [ref=e766] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B49"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Theodosiou et al., 2017" [ref=e767] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B193"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Zhang et al., 2021a" [ref=e768] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B235"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Brandenburg et al., 2021" [ref=e769] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#B27"
                    - text: ). This and other examples for TCA dependent biocatalytic reactions are discussed in the following.
                  - generic [ref=e770]:
                    - heading "3.2.1 Engineering Redox Cofactor Supply via TCA Cycle and Anaplerotic Pathways" [level=4] [ref=e771]:
                      - text: 3.2.1 Engineering Redox Cofactor Supply
                      - emphasis [ref=e772]: via
                      - text: TCA Cycle and Anaplerotic Pathways
                    - paragraph [ref=e773]:
                      - text: Apart from the PPP and the proton-translocating transhydrogenase PntAB, the malic enzyme (ME) is also considered a major source of NAD(P)H and a promising target for redox metabolism engineering (
                      - link "Figure 2" [ref=e774] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#F2"
                      - text: ). ME catalyzes the reversible oxidative decarboxylation of malate to pyruvate. MEs can reduce NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e775]: +
                      - text: ", NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e776]: +
                      - text: ", or both cofactors. In"
                      - emphasis [ref=e777]: E. coli
                      - text: ", there are 2 genes, i.e.,"
                      - emphasis [ref=e778]: maeA
                      - text: (encoding a NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e779]: +
                      - text: "-ME) and"
                      - emphasis [ref=e780]: maeB
                      - text: (encoding a NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e781]: +
                      - text: "-ME), which are part of the anaplerotic node that links glycolysis and gluconeogenesis to the TCA cycle ("
                      - link "Sauer and Eikmanns, 2005" [ref=e782] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B173"
                      - text: ). Isocitrate dehydrogenase (
                      - emphasis [ref=e783]: icd
                      - text: ) can also be an important NAD(P)H supplier (
                      - link "Sauer et al., 2004" [ref=e784] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B172"
                      - text: ). Whereas
                      - emphasis [ref=e785]: E. coli
                      - text: IDH depends on NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e786]: +
                      - text: ", eukaryotes also contain a NAD"
                      - superscript [ref=e787]: +
                      - text: "-dependent IDH playing an important role in the TCA cycle ("
                      - link "Cupp and McAlister-Henn, 1991" [ref=e788] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B44"
                      - text: ). Finally, the glyoxylate shunt can have an effect on the NADPH generation and is often manipulated to redirect fluxes. It bypasses the conversion of isocitrate to succinate and respective NAD(P)H generation in the TCA cycle. When the glyoxylate shunt is activated (e.g., by deletion of the respective repressor gene
                      - emphasis [ref=e789]: iclR
                      - text: ), more malate is formed, which in turn can be converted to pyruvate by the ME involving NADPH formation (
                      - link "Lin et al., 2013" [ref=e790] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B129"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e791]:
                      - link "Lee et al. (2011)" [ref=e792] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B111"
                      - text: overexpressed
                      - emphasis [ref=e793]: zwf
                      - text: ","
                      - emphasis [ref=e794]: icd
                      - text: ", and"
                      - emphasis [ref=e795]: maeB
                      - text: genes in order to improve NADPH-dependent GDP-l-fucose productivity in recombinant
                      - emphasis [ref=e796]: E. coli
                      - text: bearing GDP-l-fucose biosynthetic enzymes. Overexpression of
                      - emphasis [ref=e797]: icd
                      - text: resulted in 30% higher GDP-L-fucose production compared with the control, similarly as
                      - emphasis [ref=e798]: zwf
                      - text: overexpression. Overexpression of
                      - emphasis [ref=e799]: maeB
                      - text: however resulted in a 24% reduction of GDP-L-fucose production.
                      - link "Liu et al. (2018)" [ref=e800] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B131"
                      - text: overexpressed isocitrate dehydrogenase and citrate synthase genes in
                      - emphasis [ref=e801]: B. licheniformis
                      - text: DW2 to allow a high TCA cycle flux and study whether the improved ATP and NADPH levels could concomitantly enhance bacitracin biosynthesis. What they found was that the increased TCA cycle flux resulted in more energy generation and improved amino acid uptake and intracellular biosynthesis leading to larger pools of precursor amino acids for bacitracin biosynthesis. The contribution of the TCA cycle in NADPH regeneration was also confirmed by
                      - link "Chin et al. (2009)" [ref=e802] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B41"
                      - text: who worked with
                      - emphasis [ref=e803]: E. coli
                      - text: bearing NADPH-dependent xylose reductase from
                      - emphasis [ref=e804]: C. boidinii
                      - text: . They reported that succinyl-CoA synthetase (
                      - emphasis [ref=e805]: sucC
                      - text: ) deletion significantly reduced the xylitol yield on glucose provided as cosubstrate, indicating that a functional TCA cycle contributes to NADPH allocation for xylitol production.
                    - paragraph [ref=e806]:
                      - text: ME has a decisive role as major NADPH provider for fatty acid biosynthesis in oleaginous microorganisms and is often considered rate-limiting (
                      - link "Liang and Jiang, 2013" [ref=e807] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B123"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Ratledge, 2014" [ref=e808] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B165"
                      - text: ). It was shown that
                      - emphasis [ref=e809]: maeB
                      - text: overexpression in
                      - emphasis [ref=e810]: Mucor cicinelloides
                      - text: ", a commercial oil-producing fungus, resulted in a 2.5-fold increase in lipid accumulation ("
                      - link "Zhang et al., 2007" [ref=e811] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B233"
                      - text: ). This
                      - emphasis [ref=e812]: mae
                      - text: B gene also was overexpressed in the oleaginous yeast
                      - emphasis [ref=e813]: Rhodotorula glutinis
                      - text: and improved its lipid content by more than 2-fold (from 18.74 to 39.35% w/w) without affecting fatty acid profiles (
                      - link "Li et al., 2013" [ref=e814] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B122"
                      - text: ).
                      - emphasis [ref=e815]: E. coli
                      - text: could be an alternative fatty acid producer even though it provides only a small amount of the precursor malonyl-CoA.
                      - link "Meng et al. (2011)" [ref=e816] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B141"
                      - text: simulated the lipogenesis of oleaginous microorganisms in
                      - emphasis [ref=e817]: E. coli
                      - text: by coexpressing the acetyl-CoA carboxylase genes (
                      - emphasis [ref=e818]: accABCD
                      - text: ) from
                      - emphasis [ref=e819]: A. calcoaceticus
                      - text: to improve malonyl-CoA formation and the
                      - emphasis [ref=e820]: maeB
                      - text: gene from
                      - emphasis [ref=e821]: E. coli
                      - text: to improve NADPH availability. The overexpression of the
                      - emphasis [ref=e822]: acc
                      - text: genes alone improved malonyl-CoA formation resulting in a 3-fold increase in intracellular lipids, whereas
                      - emphasis [ref=e823]: maeB
                      - text: overexpression together with the addition of malate resulted in a 4-fold increase in intracellular lipids (197.74 mg g
                      - superscript [ref=e824]: −1
                      - text: ). Combining the approaches, a 5.6-fold increase of intracellular lipids was achieved (284.56 mg g
                      - superscript [ref=e825]: −1
                      - text: ). Similarly, the overexpression of ME from the oleaginous fungus
                      - emphasis [ref=e826]: Mortierell alpine
                      - text: in fatty alcohol producing
                      - emphasis [ref=e827]: S. cerevisiae
                      - text: strains lead to increased final fatty alcohol titers, with the reduction of fatty acyl-CoA to fatty alcohol requiring stoichiometric amounts of NADPH (
                      - link "Runguphan and Keasling, 2014" [ref=e828] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B168"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e829]:
                      - text: These studies implicate ME to be a key supplier of lipogenic NADPH. However,
                      - superscript [ref=e830]: "13"
                      - text: C-MFA by Wasylenko et al. revealed that, in engineered
                      - emphasis [ref=e831]: Y. lipolytica
                      - text: showing a two-fold increased fatty acid yield compared to the control strain, NADPH formation via the oxidative PPP was approximately doubled while the ME flux did not differ significantly between the two strains (
                      - link "Wasylenko et al., 2015" [ref=e832] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B215"
                      - text: ). Moreover, they found that the PPP-related NADPH formation rate was in good agreement with the estimated NADPH demand for fatty acid biosynthesis in both strains, identifying the oxidative PPP as the primary source of lipogenic NADPH in
                      - emphasis [ref=e833]: Y. lipolytica
                      - text: . Based on this finding, Qiao et al. increased lipid synthesis by engineering pathways in
                      - emphasis [ref=e834]: Y. lipolytica
                      - text: that utilize glycolytic NADH to promote the synthesis of the lipid precursors NADPH or acetyl-CoA (
                      - link "Qiao et al., 2017" [ref=e835] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B163"
                      - text: )
                      - emphasis [ref=e836]: .
                      - text: For this purpose, they constructed 13 novel strains based on a
                      - emphasis [ref=e837]: Y. lipolytica
                      - text: "variant with overexpressed acetyl-CoA carboxylase and diacylglyceride acyltransferase. They concluded that at least three strategies can be applied to convert most of the cytosolic NADH to NADPH leading to overall process yield improvement: 1) high-level expression of the NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e838]: +
                      - text: "-dependent glyceraldhyde-3-phosphate dehydrogenase (GAPDH) from"
                      - emphasis [ref=e839]: C. acetobutylicum
                      - text: ", 2) overexpression of NAD"
                      - superscript [ref=e840]: +
                      - text: kinase, and 3) introduction of cytosolic NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e841]: +
                      - text: "-dependent malic enzyme. Interestingly, the conversion of NADH to NADPH reduced the O"
                      - subscript [ref=e842]: "2"
                      - text: demand of the culture enabling higher cell densities with the same aeration capacity.
                    - paragraph [ref=e843]:
                      - text: MFA has shown that the competition for NAD(P)H between redox biocatalysis and the energy metabolism becomes critical during asymmetric styrene epoxidation catalyzed by growing
                      - emphasis [ref=e844]: E. coli
                      - text: containing recombinant NADH-dependent styrene monooxygenase (
                      - link "Bühler et al., 2008" [ref=e845] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B29"
                      - text: ). Engineering TCA-cycle regulation allowed increased TCA-cycle activities, a delay of acetate formation, and enhanced NAD(P)H yields during batch cultivation (
                      - link "Kuhn et al., 2013" [ref=e846] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B101"
                      - text: ). This, however, could not improve whole-cell styrene epoxidation activities, whereas elevated product concentrations were found to cause a significantly increased NAD(P)H demand and a compromised efficiency of metabolic operation. Higher styrene epoxidation activities however were obtained with constitutively solvent-tolerant
                      - emphasis [ref=e847]: Pseudomonas taiwanensis
                      - text: VLB120ΔCΔ
                      - emphasis [ref=e848]: ttgV
                      - text: (
                      - link "Volmer et al., 2014" [ref=e849] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B209"
                      - text: ) as host strain with an inherently higher glucose uptake rate and TCA cycle activity (
                      - link "Volmer et al., 2019" [ref=e850] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B208"
                      - text: ).
                  - generic [ref=e851]:
                    - heading "3.2.2 Engineering Cosubstrate Supply via the TCA Cycle" [level=4] [ref=e852]:
                      - text: 3.2.2 Engineering Cosubstrate Supply
                      - emphasis [ref=e853]: via
                      - text: the TCA Cycle
                    - paragraph [ref=e854]:
                      - text: As mentioned above, synthetically interesting biocatalytic reactions can directly depend on central carbon metabolites that serve as cosubstrates. The hydroxylation of L-proline to trans-4-hydroxy-L-proline (hyp) using an α-KG dependent L-proline-4-hydroxylase (P4H) is such an example (
                      - link "Shibasaki et al., 2000" [ref=e855] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B183"
                      - text: ). The reaction catalyzed by recombinant
                      - emphasis [ref=e856]: E. coli
                      - text: cells bearing P4H from
                      - emphasis [ref=e857]: Dactylosporangium
                      - text: sp. strain RH1 requires stoichiometric amounts of the TCA cycle intermediate α-KG. In this hydroxylation reaction, one atom of molecular oxygen is introduced into L-proline to yield hyp, while the other oxygen atom is used for oxidative decarboxylation of α-KG giving rise to succinate and CO
                      - subscript [ref=e858]: "2"
                      - text: . This decarboxylation added a shortcut of the TCA. For the synthesis of hyp from proline, a commercially used whole-cell process has been reported (
                      - link "Shibasaki et al., 2000" [ref=e859] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B183"
                      - text: ) and the interdependency of process conditions, host metabolism, and catalyst performance was resolved (
                      - link "Theodosiou et al., 2015" [ref=e860] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B194"
                      - text: ). In an attempt to evaluate TCA cycle engineering strategies to enforce and increase α-KG flux through P4H,
                      - link "Theodosiou et al. (2017)" [ref=e861] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B193"
                      - text: deleted the TCA-cycle genes
                      - emphasis [ref=e862]: sucA
                      - text: (
                      - emphasis [ref=e863]: α
                      - text: "-KG dehydrogenase E1 subunit) or"
                      - emphasis [ref=e864]: sucC
                      - text: (succinyl-CoA synthetase β subunit) together with
                      - emphasis [ref=e865]: aceA
                      - text: (isocitrate lyase), disrupting both glyoxylate shunt and TCA cycle (
                      - link "Figure 2" [ref=e866] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#F2"
                      - text: ), in a proline degradation-deficient
                      - emphasis [ref=e867]: E. coli
                      - text: strain (Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e868]: putA
                      - text: ) expressing the
                      - emphasis [ref=e869]: p4h
                      - text: gene. Whereas the Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e870]: sucC
                      - text: Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e871]: aceA
                      - text: Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e872]: putA
                      - text: strain grew in minimal medium in the absence of P4H, relying on the activity of fumarate reductase, growth of the Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e873]: sucA
                      - text: Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e874]: aceA
                      - text: Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e875]: putA
                      - text: strictly depended on P4H activity, thus coupling growth to proline hydroxylation. Employing a Na
                      - superscript [ref=e876]: +
                      - text: /L-proline transporter (
                      - emphasis [ref=e877]: putP
                      - text: gene) in the Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e878]: sucA
                      - text: Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e879]: aceA
                      - text: Δ
                      - emphasis [ref=e880]: putA
                      - text: strain
                      - emphasis [ref=e881]: ","
                      - text: the specific proline hydroxylation rate doubled (
                      - link "Theodosiou et al., 2017" [ref=e882] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B193"
                      - text: ). In a similar approach,
                      - link "Smirnov et al. (2010)" [ref=e883] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B187"
                      - text: produced (2S,3R, 4S)-4-hydroxyisoleucine from L-isoleucine by means of recombinant
                      - emphasis [ref=e884]: E. coli
                      - text: carrying an α-ketoglutarate-dependent L-isoleucine dioxygenase (IDO) (
                      - link "Kodera et al., 2009" [ref=e885] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B98"
                      - text: ). Also here, IDO introduction was able to shunt the TCA cycle in an
                      - emphasis [ref=e886]: E. coli
                      - text: mutant with both glyoxylate shunt and TCA-cycle disrupted, forcing metabolic flux through the hydroxylation reaction. The same engineering was successfully applied for the synthesis of G-7-aminodeacetoxycephalosporanic acid (G-7-ADCA) from penicillin G (
                      - link "Lin et al., 2015" [ref=e887] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B127"
                      - text: ). In that case, an
                      - emphasis [ref=e888]: E. coli
                      - text: strain expressing α-KG-dependent deacetoxycephalosporin C synthase (DAOCS) was used to reconstitute the TCA cycle. Additional engineering (
                      - emphasis [ref=e889]: poxB
                      - text: deletion and
                      - emphasis [ref=e890]: acs
                      - text: overexpression) reduced acetate accumulation in order to prevent medium acidification and carbon loss. Together with the deletion of the host
                      - emphasis [ref=e891]: β
                      - text: "-lactamase involved in penicillin G and G-7-ADCA degradation, the G-7-ADCA titer could be increased 11-fold to 29.01 mM. Interestingly, the constructed vector systems did not require antibiotic selection, as survival depended on the expression of the dioxygenase encoded on the vector."
                    - paragraph [ref=e892]:
                      - text: Whilethe exploitation of α-KG dependent dioxygenases in heterotrophic bacteria requires organic carbon to fuel the TCA cycle, cyanobacterial reliance on CO
                      - subscript [ref=e893]: "2"
                      - text: offers a promising sustainable alternative (
                      - link "Figure 3" [ref=e894] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#F3"
                      - text: ). The first exploitation of the cyanobacterial TCA cycle was reported in 1994 for the production of ethylene, a widely used feedstock in plastic industry, by introducing the
                      - emphasis [ref=e895]: efe
                      - text: gene from
                      - emphasis [ref=e896]: Pseudomonas syringae pv. phaseolicola
                      - text: PK2 into
                      - emphasis [ref=e897]: S. elongatus
                      - text: PCC 7942 (
                      - link "Fukuda et al., 1994" [ref=e898] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B56"
                      - text: ). Since then, metabolic engineering and synthetic biology of cyanobacteria greatly advanced photoautotrophic ethylene production as recently reviewed by
                      - link "Kallio et al. (2021)" [ref=e899] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B82"
                      - text: . Regarding amino acid hydroxylation with high potential in food and pharmaceutical industries, the work of
                      - link "Brandenburg et al. (2021)" [ref=e900] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B27"
                      - text: describes the first successful example of photosynthetic hyp production directly from CO
                      - subscript [ref=e901]: "2"
                      - text: ", by genetic engineering of the cyanobacterium"
                      - emphasis [ref=e902]: Synechocystis
                      - text: sp. PCC 6803 bearing the
                      - emphasis [ref=e903]: p4h
                      - text: gene from
                      - emphasis [ref=e904]: Dactylosporangium
                      - text: .
                    - figure "Coupling of α-ketoglutarate dependent oxygenases to phototrophic and heterotrophic metabolism. These enzymes catalyze a remarkable range of reactions, such as hydroxylation, desaturation, and epoxidation. They utilize iron (II) as the metallo-cofactor and α-ketoglutarate as the co-substrate that is oxidatively decarboxylated to succinate and CO2." [ref=e905]:
                      - heading "FIGURE 3." [level=5] [ref=e906]
                      - paragraph [ref=e907]:
                        - link "FIGURE 3 🔍" [ref=e908] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=9043136_fbioe-10-855715-g003.jpg
                          - img "FIGURE 3" [ref=e909]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e911] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/F3/
                      - paragraph [ref=e913]:
                        - text: Coupling of α-ketoglutarate dependent oxygenases to phototrophic and heterotrophic metabolism. These enzymes catalyze a remarkable range of reactions, such as hydroxylation, desaturation, and epoxidation. They utilize iron (II) as the metallo-cofactor and α-ketoglutarate as the co-substrate that is oxidatively decarboxylated to succinate and CO
                        - subscript [ref=e914]: "2"
                        - text: .
                - generic [ref=e915]:
                  - heading "3.3 Engineering Phototrophic Metabolic Modules for Whole-Cell Biocatalysis" [level=3] [ref=e916]
                  - generic [ref=e917]:
                    - heading "3.3.1 Direct Utilization of Light" [level=4] [ref=e918]
                    - paragraph [ref=e919]:
                      - text: Biocatalytic redox reactions depend not only on suitable enzymes, but also on efficient and sustainable redox cofactor supply. To find the optimal electron transfer partner, both the redox potential and the supply rate of this carrier have to be considered. Most commonly, NADH- or NADPH-dependent enzymes are used. However, photosynthetic cells offer various other electron carriers, such as ferredoxin (Fd), the primary electron acceptor of photosystem I.
                      - link "Figure 4" [ref=e920] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#F4"
                      - text: displays the photosynthetic electron transport chain (PETC) and the variety of electron carriers involved as well as possible homo-/heterologous electron sinks.
                    - figure [ref=e921]:
                      - heading "FIGURE 4." [level=5] [ref=e922]
                      - paragraph [ref=e923]:
                        - link "FIGURE 4 🔍" [ref=e924] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=9043136_fbioe-10-855715-g004.jpg
                          - img "FIGURE 4" [ref=e925]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e927] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/F4/
                      - paragraph [ref=e929]:
                        - text: "Tapping the reductive potential of photosynthesis. Green: components of the photosynthetic electron transfer chain (PETC); yellow: internal electron sinks; blue: (native) hydrogenase (Hox); red: (recombinant) redox enzymes. Electrons derived from water splitting enter the photosynthetic electron transport chain at photosystem II, where energy from light quanta promotes them to a higher excitation state (a more negative potential). This excited state can be used for photochemistry, i.e., the reduction of the primary acceptor Q"
                        - subscript [ref=e930]: A
                        - text: /Q
                        - subscript [ref=e931]: B
                        - text: ", followed by electron transport over plastoquinone (PQ) and cytochromes (Cyt"
                        - subscript [ref=e932]: b/d
                        - text: and Cyt
                        - subscript [ref=e933]: c
                        - text: ) along a gradient of redox potentials. Photosystem I again absorbs light energy and constitutes a reducing agent strong enough to transfer electrons to Ferredoxin (Fd). The minimal redox potentials in photosystems are not drawn to scale. Ferredoxin-NADPH-reductase (FNR) allocates a big share of these electrons to NADPH, which either can be used for C-fixation or can be converted to NADH by transhydrogenases (TH). Alternatively, electrons can be distributed to N- and S-assimilation (Fd-nitrate-reductase NarB, Fd-nitrite-reductase NirA, and Fd-sulfite-reductase SiR), regulatory elements (e.g., Fd-thioredoxin-reductase FTR), or back to photosystem I, referred to as cyclic electron transfer (CET). At different points of the PETC, reductive stress can be released by transferring electrons to acceptors like the flavodiiron proteins Flv2/4 and Flv1/3 and respiratory oxidases like the PQ-terminal oxidase PTOX, Cyt
                        - subscript [ref=e934]: b/d
                        - text: "- oxidase Cyd, and Cyt"
                        - subscript [ref=e935]: c
                        - text: "-oxidase Cox. Redox potentials of electron sinks are not drawn to scale. So far, the reductive potential of photosynthesis was tapped at or downstream of PS I, by fusion constructs (dotted line), at the level of Fd (Hox and CYP450), and at the level of NADPH (Baeyer-Villiger monooxygenase BVMO and ene-reductase Yqjm)."
                    - paragraph [ref=e936]:
                      - text: Due to the relatively low redox potential of Fd (
                      - link "Wlodarczyk et al., 2015" [ref=e937] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B218"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Berepiki et al., 2016" [ref=e938] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B18"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Hoschek et al., 2019a" [ref=e939] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B66"
                      - text: ), application of Fd-dependent enzymes seems to be highly promising. Several Fd-dependent oxygenases have been applied and specific activities up to 40 U g
                      - subscript [ref=e940]: CDW
                      - superscript [ref=e941]: −1
                      - text: were achieved. Both enzyme specific and host intrinsic Fds have been found to shuttle electrons, with differing efficiency. Phototrophs contain multiple Fds with, e.g., at least nine Fd in the model cyanobacterium
                      - emphasis [ref=e942]: Synechocystis
                      - text: sp. PCC 6803 (
                      - link "Cassier-Chauvat and Chauvat, 2014" [ref=e943] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B35"
                      - text: ). This complicates the prediction of electron transfer efficiencies towards Fd-dependent enzymes. NADPH (
                      - link "Böhmer et al., 2017" [ref=e944] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B24"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Tüllinghoff et al., 2022" [ref=e945] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B200"
                      - text: ), NADH, flavodoxins or plastochinones also have the potential to be used as electron donors. Enzymes depending on NADPH as a main product of the light reaction may benefit from the relatively large NADPH pool, but may suffer from competition with electron consuming metabolic routes, especially CO
                      - subscript [ref=e946]: "2"
                      - text: fixation and nitrate assimilation (
                      - link "Mellor et al., 2017" [ref=e947] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B139"
                      - text: ). A study investigating the most suitable carrier for a CYP450 showed that 1) the type of available electron carrier determines the biocatalytic reaction rate and 2) this rate profits from electron carrier-CYP450 fusion (
                      - link "Mellor et al., 2019" [ref=e948] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B140"
                      - text: ). Methods to improve intracellular CYP450 catalysis, including enzyme-, redox-partner-, and substrate engineering recently were nicely reviewed by
                      - link "Li et al. (2020)" [ref=e949] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B121"
                      - text: . An improved connection to the PETC has the potential to significantly increase production rates, as shown for the linkage of a CYP450 to the thylakoid membrane (
                      - link "Lassen et al., 2014a" [ref=e950] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B107"
                      - text: ). A direct connection to the photosynthetic apparatus, as demonstrated for hydrogenases (
                      - link "Appel et al., 2020" [ref=e951] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B7"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Kanygin et al., 2020" [ref=e952] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B85"
                      - text: ), allowed a direct electron transfer, mitigating unwanted electron leakage. As a strategy to avoid intracellular competition for redox equivalents, FNR affinity for ferredoxin can be attenuated, thereby making more ferredoxin available for target reactions, e.g., hydrogen formation catalyzed by Fd-accepting hydrogenases (
                      - link "Kannchen et al., 2020" [ref=e953] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B84"
                      - text: ). The plastochinone pool in principle constitutes another attractive point to tap reducing power from photosynthesis, with native transfer to the terminal oxidase as an example (
                      - link "Feilke et al., 2017" [ref=e954] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B52"
                      - text: ). Its relatively positive redox potential however limits the amount of reactions to be potentially fueled by plastochinone.
                    - paragraph [ref=e955]:
                      - text: The implementation of biocatalytic reactions in phototrophs mostly constituted an iterative process. Several CYP450, BVMOs, reductases, hydrogenases, etc., have been applied with specific activities up to 150 U g
                      - subscript [ref=e956]: CDW
                      - superscript [ref=e957]: −1
                      - text: as summarized in
                      - link "Table 2" [ref=e958] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#T2"
                      - text: . Interestingly, electron drainage
                      - emphasis [ref=e959]: via
                      - text: biotransformation reactions appeared to cause an ATP/NADPH imbalance, (possibly) affecting both the biocatalytic reaction and the host metabolism. The introduction of heterologous pathways relying on C-fixation and reducing power showed a way to avoid such imbalance (
                      - link "Berepiki et al., 2018" [ref=e960] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B17"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Santos-Merino et al., 2021" [ref=e961] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B171"
                      - text: ). Thus, the balance of sources and sinks should be considered. As with heterotrophic microbes, substrate and product inhibition, toxicity, and volatility also have to be considered with substrate supply as a biochemical engineering target (
                      - link "Lin and Tao, 2017" [ref=e962] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B128"
                      - text: ). For BVMO catalysis in
                      - emphasis [ref=e963]: Synechocystis
                      - text: sp. PCC 6803 for instance, kinetic constraints have been found to be more relevant and thus overlay photosynthesis-related effects such as light limitation (
                      - link "Tüllinghoff et al., 2022" [ref=e964] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B200"
                      - text: ).
                    - generic [ref=e965]:
                      - heading "TABLE 2." [level=5] [ref=e966]
                      - paragraph [ref=e968]:
                        - text: Recombinant redox biotransformations in cyanobacteria and microalgae. Adapted from
                        - link "Jodlbauer et al. (2021)" [ref=e969] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#B78"
                        - text: und updated.

                      - generic [ref=e1108]:
                        - generic [ref=e1109]:
                          - superscript [ref=e1110]: a
                          - paragraph [ref=e1111]: —Calculated from substrate consumption.
                        - generic [ref=e1112]:

                          - paragraph [ref=e1114]:
                            - text: —Regenerated by formate reduction by

                            - text: —Estimation based on (
                            - link "Fan et al., 2017" [ref=e1119] [cursor=pointer]:
                              - /url: "#B50"
