                        - paragraph [ref=e1125]: H-NAME—ω-hydroxynanoic acid methyl ester, Fd—Ferredoxin.
                    - paragraph [ref=e1126]:
                      - text: In contrast to cyanobacteria, which have been used in various studies for biocatalysis, examples employing green algae are scarce (
                      - link "Zheng et al., 2018" [ref=e1127] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B237"
                      - text: ), which is mostly due to the limited molecular biology toolbox for such strains.
                      - emphasis [ref=e1128]: Chlorella vulgaris
                      - text: or
                      - emphasis [ref=e1129]: Chlamydomonas reinhardtii
                      - text: were recently applied as whole-cell biocatalysts, using the photosynthetic reduction power for either dehalogenations (
                      - link "Khan et al., 2021" [ref=e1130] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B91"
                      - text: ) or aliphatic amine formation via an amine dehydrogenase (
                      - link "Löwe et al., 2018" [ref=e1131] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B132"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Gröger, 2019" [ref=e1132] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B57"
                      - text: ). The presented examples indicate a limited applicability of phototrophs for NADH-dependent reactions, which only indirectly couple to the photosynthetic light reaction. Application of such enzymes in cyanobacteria/green algae requires either a change in enzymatic cofactor preference (
                      - link "Park and Choi, 2017" [ref=e1133] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B154"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Wang et al., 2017" [ref=e1134] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B212"
                      - text: ) or redirection of electrons towards NADH. Implementation of NADH-dependent nitrate assimilation led to enhanced NADH production, supporting the target pathways and finally the NADPH-dependent butanol formation (
                      - link "Purdy et al., 2022" [ref=e1135] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B162"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e1136]:
                      - text: Besides providing reduction power, the photosynthetic light reaction can supply O
                      - subscript [ref=e1137]: "2"
                      - text: and thus overcome one major limitation in the application of oxygenases in heterotrophs. Theoretical calculations indicate that optimally aerated growing heterotrophic cells can reach productivities up to 3.5 g L
                      - superscript [ref=e1138]: −1
                      - text: h
                      - superscript [ref=e1139]: −1
                      - text: without oxygen limitation (
                      - link "Pedersen et al., 2015" [ref=e1140] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B157"
                      - text: ). For CYP catalysis in cyanobacteria (
                      - link "Hoschek et al., 2018" [ref=e1141] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B69"
                      - text: ), external O
                      - subscript [ref=e1142]: "2"
                      - text: supply could even be completely omitted and the reaction be fueled by photosynthesis-derived O
                      - subscript [ref=e1143]: "2"
                      - text: . Additionally, an indirect O
                      - subscript [ref=e1144]: "2"
                      - text: supply was demonstrated in a mixed species approach, in which phototrophs provided heterotrophic whole-cell biocatalysts with O
                      - subscript [ref=e1145]: "2"
                      - text: (
                      - link "Hoschek et al., 2019b" [ref=e1146] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B68"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Heuschkel et al., 2019" [ref=e1147] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B62"
                      - text: ). Furthermore, photosynthetic
                      - emphasis [ref=e1148]: in situ
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e1149]: "2"
                      - text: supply makes application of other O
                      - subscript [ref=e1150]: "2"
                      - text: "-dependent enzymes not relying on reduction power conceivable, e.g., alcohol oxidases, amine oxidases, laccases, and dioxygenases. The oxidation of alcohols to aldehydes or ketones by alcohol oxidases is of particular interest for the production of flavors and fragrances. Examples include benzaldehyde (bitter almond), cinnamaldehyde (cinnamon), octanal (citrus), 2-heptanone (banana), ionones (rose), and vanillin (vanilla) ("
                      - link "Berger, 2009" [ref=e1151] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B20"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Berger, 2015" [ref=e1152] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B19"
                      - text: ). Laccases, which gained interest in recent years (
                      - link "Bassanini et al., 2021" [ref=e1153] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B13"
                      - text: ), are present in phototrophic species and have the potential to be used for degradation processes (
                      - link "Afreen et al., 2017" [ref=e1154] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B1"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Liang et al., 2018" [ref=e1155] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B124"
                      - text: ).
                  - generic [ref=e1156]:
                    - heading "3.3.2 Calculations Regarding Light-to-Product Efficiency" [level=4] [ref=e1157]
                    - paragraph [ref=e1158]: "For the envisioned photosynthesis-driven redox biocatalysis, the light-to-product efficiency is of special interest. Major questions to be answered, are:"
                    - list [ref=e1159]:
                      - listitem [ref=e1160]:
                        - paragraph [ref=e1161]: 1) How efficiently does the biocatalyst use the incident light? What are the resulting electron transfer rates (ETRs)?
                      - listitem [ref=e1162]:
                        - paragraph [ref=e1163]: 2) At which point can photosynthesis be tapped with what efficiency?
                      - listitem [ref=e1164]:
                        - paragraph [ref=e1165]: 3) Which strains or strain character traits are desirable to improve photosynthesis-driven biotechnology?
                    - paragraph [ref=e1166]:
                      - text: The light-to-biomass efficiency is maximal at the light saturation point E
                      - subscript [ref=e1167]: k
                      - text: ", where the incident light is just sufficient to supply the metabolism with the reductive power needed, e.g., for biomass production. In natural environments, phototrophic organisms face a “light usage dilemma” ("
                      - link "Wilhelm et al., 2021" [ref=e1168] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B217"
                      - text: ), consisting of the incident light being either below or above E
                      - subscript [ref=e1169]: k
                      - text: ", impairing overall photosynthetic efficiency. In fact, 82% of the incident photosynthetically active radiation (PAR) reach the surface with light intensities above E"
                      - subscript [ref=e1170]: k
                      - text: (
                      - link "Wilhelm et al., 2021" [ref=e1171] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B217"
                      - text: ). Consequently, the photosynthetic apparatus is evolutionarily optimized to handle over-excitation rather than to maximize light usage efficiency.
                    - paragraph [ref=e1172]:
                      - text: "Within the photosynthetic apparatus, (excess) energy is lost at different levels, often by mechanisms important to protect the apparatus from over-excitation: 1) Depending on their pigmentation, photosynthetic organisms absorb only a share of PAR, expressed as the share of photosynthetically absorbed quanta Q"
                      - subscript [ref=e1173]: phar
                      - text: . 2) At photosystem II (PSII), a part of the energy is dissipated via heat or fluorescence emission and non-photochemical quenching (NPQ), which decreases the operative quantum yield at PSII (Ф
                      - subscript [ref=e1174]: PSII
                      - text: ). Ф
                      - subscript [ref=e1175]: PSII
                      - text: can be assessed
                      - emphasis [ref=e1176]: via
                      - text: pulse-amplitude-modulation (PAM) fluorometry to calculate the ETR in PSII. 3) Alternative electron flux (AEF) via terminal oxidases and Mehler- or Mehler-type reactions further compromises the light-to-biomass efficiency. As a result, only a fraction of the incident light energy can be used for biomass formation or productive (biocatalytic) reactions. To illustrate these theoretical considerations,
                      - link "Table 3" [ref=e1177] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#T3"
                      - text: summarizes relevant rates of O
                      - subscript [ref=e1178]: "2"
                      - text: evolution, electron supply, and growth for typical phototrophic microorganisms.
                    - generic [ref=e1179]:
                      - heading "TABLE 3." [level=5] [ref=e1180]

                          - row "Chlamydomonas reinhardtii 0.15 ± 0.01 (Langner et al., 2009) 1.08 ± 0.06 c (Langner et al., 2009) 0.059 (Boyle and Morgan, 2009)" [ref=e1225]:

                              - emphasis [ref=e1227]: Chlamydomonas reinhardtii

                              - link "Langner et al., 2009" [ref=e1229] [cursor=pointer]:
                                - /url: "#B104"
                              - text: )

                              - text: 1.08 ± 0.06
                              - link "c" [ref=e1231] [cursor=pointer]:
                                - /url: "#Tfn7"
                                - superscript [ref=e1232]: c
                              - text: (
                              - link "Langner et al., 2009" [ref=e1233] [cursor=pointer]:
                                - /url: "#B104"

                            - cell "0.059 (Boyle and Morgan, 2009)" [ref=e1234]:
                              - text: 0.059 (
                              - link "Boyle and Morgan, 2009" [ref=e1235] [cursor=pointer]:
                                - /url: "#B26"
                              - text: )
                          - row "Chlorella vulgaris 0.11 ± 0.01 (Wagner et al., 2006) 0.94 ± 0.02 c (Wagner et al., 2006) 0.07 (Schuurmans et al., 2015)" [ref=e1236]:
                            - cell "Chlorella vulgaris" [ref=e1237]:
                              - emphasis [ref=e1238]: Chlorella vulgaris
                            - cell "0.11 ± 0.01 (Wagner et al., 2006)" [ref=e1239]:
                              - text: 0.11 ± 0.01 (
                              - link "Wagner et al., 2006" [ref=e1240] [cursor=pointer]:
                                - /url: "#B211"
                              - text: )
                            - cell "0.94 ± 0.02 c (Wagner et al., 2006)" [ref=e1241]:
                              - text: 0.94 ± 0.02
                              - link "c" [ref=e1242] [cursor=pointer]:
                                - /url: "#Tfn7"
                                - superscript [ref=e1243]: c
                              - text: (
                              - link "Wagner et al., 2006" [ref=e1244] [cursor=pointer]:
                                - /url: "#B211"

                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e1248] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: table/T3/
                      - generic [ref=e1249]:
                        - generic [ref=e1250]:
                          - superscript [ref=e1251]: a
                          - paragraph [ref=e1252]: Depending on light conditions.
                        - generic [ref=e1253]:
                          - superscript [ref=e1254]: b
                          - paragraph [ref=e1255]:
                            - text: Assessed
                            - emphasis [ref=e1256]: via
                            - text: NADPH-fluorescence measurements.
                        - generic [ref=e1257]:
                          - superscript [ref=e1258]: c
                          - paragraph [ref=e1259]:
                            - text: Assessed
                            - emphasis [ref=e1260]: via
                            - text: Chl
                            - subscript [ref=e1261]: a
                            - text: measurements at PS II.
                    - paragraph [ref=e1262]:
                      - text: Due to the energy losses along the photosynthetic electron transfer chain, it is obvious that the closer a reaction is situated to the water splitting, the higher are electron supply rate and light use efficiencies. About 50–60% (
                      - link "Wilhelm et al., 2021" [ref=e1263] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B217"
                      - text: ) of the light energy can in principle be tapped at PSII or directly downstream to it, at the plastoquinone pool, where losses only result from heat and fluorescence dissipation and non-photochemical quenching. Tapping reductive power at PS I or at its acceptor side (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1264]: via
                      - text: Fd or NADPH as described above) involves electron losses to respiratory elements, like oxidases, or Mehler- and Mehler-type reactions. Taken together, these losses account for 20–30%, reducing the light use efficiency to 15–20%. Beside these theoretical efficiencies, the redox potentials of tapped PETC cofactors or electron carriers are of practical importance as reviewed by
                      - link "Mellor et al. (2017)" [ref=e1265] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B139"
                      - text: ": PSII [∼−110 mV in"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1266]: Synechocystis
                      - text: sp. PCC 6803 (
                      - link "Allakhverdiev et al., 2011" [ref=e1267] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B3"
                      - text: )] and plastoquinone [+80 to +110 mV (
                      - link "Antal et al., 2013" [ref=e1268] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B6"
                      - text: )] have a by far lower reductive power than already used electron carriers like Fd [−430 mV to −300 mV (
                      - link "Cammack et al., 1977" [ref=e1269] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B32"
                      - text: )] and NADPH (−320 mV), which makes the latter ones highly suitable electron donors for redox biocatalysis.
                    - paragraph [ref=e1270]:
                      - text: In agreement with these theoretical considerations regarding light-to-product efficiencies, potential hosts for light-driven biotechnology require special character traits to ensure powerful and robust processes. High-light- and high-temperature-tolerances are especially important for outdoor applications. Adaptive laboratory evolution (ALE) in combination with systems biology approaches, were successfully applied for
                      - emphasis [ref=e1271]: Chlamydomonas, Synechocystis
                      - text: ", and"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1272]: Synechococcus
                      - text: (
                      - link "Zhang et al., 2021b" [ref=e1273] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B229"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Srivastava et al., 2021" [ref=e1274] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B189"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "LaPanse et al., 2021" [ref=e1275] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B105"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Dann et al., 2021" [ref=e1276] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B45"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Mukherjee et al., 2020" [ref=e1277] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B143"
                      - text: ).
                      - emphasis [ref=e1278]: Synechocystis
                      - text: sp. PCC 6803, for instance, was adapted to cope with additional (light) energy on account of a higher light harvesting capacity (
                      - link "Dann et al., 2021" [ref=e1279] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B45"
                      - text: ), auguring well for an effective use of high light intensities. Relatively low growth rates constitute another obstacle for photo-biotechnology (
                      - link "Table 3" [ref=e1280] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#T3"
                      - text: ). Here, the fast growing
                      - emphasis [ref=e1281]: Synechococcus elongatus
                      - text: UTEX 2973 (
                      - link "Ungerer et al., 2018" [ref=e1282] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B202"
                      - text: ) and the recently described
                      - emphasis [ref=e1283]: Synechococcus
                      - text: sp. PCC 11901 represent a significant progress (
                      - link "Włodarczyk et al., 2020" [ref=e1284] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B219"
                      - text: ). For the production of fuels or chemicals with (phototrophic) whole-cell factories, solvent tolerance is another desirable trait, for which considerable ALE-based progress recently has been reported for
                      - emphasis [ref=e1285]: Synechococcus
                      - text: (
                      - link "Srivastava et al., 2021" [ref=e1286] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B189"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e1287]:
                      - text: As mentioned above, phototrophs experience light excess under normal/natural conditions and have evolved multiple mechanisms for excessive energy dissipation (
                      - link "Muramatsu and Hihara, 2012" [ref=e1288] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B144"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Canonico et al., 2021" [ref=e1289] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B34"
                      - text: ). Approaches are needed to avoid such energy loss by overflow valves and to control electron flow. Several studies tackled this aspect in cyanobacteria and green algae. The major protection mechanisms in cyanobacteria are cyclic electron flow around PSI consuming up to 35% of the photosynthetically captured electrons (
                      - link "Theune et al., 2020" [ref=e1290] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B195"
                      - text: ), flavodiiron proteins, the orange carotenoid protein as main energy dissipater in cyanobacterial antenna systems, and adaptations of the photosynthetic apparatus, e.g., state transitions, antenna sizes, etc. Flavodiiron proteins (
                      - link "Bao et al., 2017" [ref=e1291] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B12"
                      - text: ) were targeted in several studies (
                      - link "Allahverdiyeva et al., 2015" [ref=e1292] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B2"
                      - text: ). Knock out strategies increased electron flow and desired product formation (
                      - link "Thiel et al., 2019" [ref=e1293] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B196"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Assil-Companioni et al., 2020" [ref=e1294] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B10"
                      - text: ). As recently reviewed (
                      - link "Bassi and Dall'Osto, 2021" [ref=e1295] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B14"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Magdaong and Blankenship, 2018" [ref=e1296] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B137"
                      - text: ), numerous mechanisms protect green algae from photoinhibition, which should be tackled in a holistic approach.
                  - generic [ref=e1297]:
                    - heading "3.3.3 Biotechnological Application/Engineering of Phototrophs" [level=4] [ref=e1298]
                    - paragraph [ref=e1299]:
                      - text: Several challenges have to be addressed to apply phototrophic organisms for production purposes. Respective processes are often hindered by light limitation, inefficient light utilization, low biomass concentrations, and low production rates. As light supply is a key aspect and a major limiting factor for high-density cultivation of phototrophs, measures are needed to improve light supply and utilization (
                      - link "Stephens et al., 2021" [ref=e1300] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B190"
                      - text: ). Major factors are the limited absorption capacities and self-shading effects in outdoor applications (
                      - link "Socher et al., 2016" [ref=e1301] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B188"
                      - text: ). Systems and synthetic biology approaches aim at the engineering of strains with improved photosynthetic performance based on, e.g., improved photosystems, optimal protein allocation, and/or efficient carbon concentration and fixation (
                      - link "Burnap, 2015" [ref=e1302] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B30"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Ort et al., 2015" [ref=e1303] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B150"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Jahn et al., 2018" [ref=e1304] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B74"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Luan et al., 2020" [ref=e1305] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B133"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Miao et al., 2020" [ref=e1306] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B142"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Roussou et al., 2021" [ref=e1307] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B166"
                      - text: ). Strategies include the reduction of antenna systems for more efficient light absorption in high-density cultures (
                      - link "Kirst et al., 2014" [ref=e1308] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B96"
                      - text: ), attenuation of protection mechanisms (
                      - link "Allahverdiyeva et al., 2015" [ref=e1309] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B2"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Bassi and Dall'Osto, 2021" [ref=e1310] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B14"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Kirilovsky et al., 2014" [ref=e1311] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B94"
                      - text: ), and streamlining of carbon, energy, and redox metabolism (
                      - link "Veaudor et al., 2020" [ref=e1312] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B206"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Roussou et al., 2021" [ref=e1313] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B166"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Santos-Merino et al., 2021" [ref=e1314] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B171"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Zhou et al., 2021" [ref=e1315] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B238"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Jaiswal et al., 2022" [ref=e1316] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B75"
                      - text: ). Furthermore, operating costs and costs for product removal have to be considered. Substrate and product toxicity constitute a general critical factor for industrial application, which can be mitigated by
                      - emphasis [ref=e1317]: in situ
                      - text: product removal (ISPR) concepts. As an example, a two-liquid-phase system has been demonstrated to stabilize a cyanobacterial CYP450-based process by reducing substrate toxicity and volatility (
                      - link "Hoschek et al., 2019a" [ref=e1318] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B66"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e1319]:
                      - text: Conventional photobioreactor systems are often limited by low cell densities (
                      - link "Chanquia et al., 2021" [ref=e1320] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B37"
                      - text: ). Therefore, innovative approaches to overcome light limitation have been developed recently (
                      - link "Johnson et al., 2018" [ref=e1321] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B79"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Kirnev et al., 2020" [ref=e1322] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B95"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Chanquia et al., 2021" [ref=e1323] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B37"
                      - text: ), including bioreactors with internal illumination (
                      - link "Heining et al., 2015" [ref=e1324] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B60"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Hobisch et al., 2021" [ref=e1325] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B63"
                      - text: ), biofilm-based concepts (
                      - link "Li et al., 2017" [ref=e1326] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B118"
                      - text: ), and cell immobilization approaches (
                      - link "Léonard et al., 2010" [ref=e1327] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B114"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Polakovič et al., 2017" [ref=e1328] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B160"
                      - text: ). Biofilm bioreactors enable high surface to volume ratios and thus high biomass concentrations and high light utilization efficiency. Bioreactors with internal light supply enabled a reduced light limitation of suspended cells and a more than two-fold increase in volumetric productivity for photosynthesis-driven 2-methylmaleimide reduction (
                      - link "Hobisch et al., 2021" [ref=e1329] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B63"
                      - text: ). Further, natural or synthetic consortia recently attracted attention (
                      - link "Hays et al., 2017" [ref=e1330] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B59"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Weiss et al., 2017" [ref=e1331] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B216"
                      - text: ), offering benefits regarding process stability, multistep enzyme cascades, cofactor supply, and light utilization (
                      - link "Fedeson et al., 2021" [ref=e1332] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B51"
                      - text: ).
                      - link "Hoschek et al. (2019b)" [ref=e1333] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B68"
                      - text: demonstrated that a two species biofilm approach enables a process duration of several weeks for cyclohexane hydroxylation at a rate of up to 3.76 g
                      - subscript [ref=e1334]: cyclohexanol
                      - text: m
                      - superscript [ref=e1335]: −2
                      - text: day
                      - superscript [ref=e1336]: −1
                      - text: with reduction equivalents and O
                      - subscript [ref=e1337]: "2"
                      - text: provided by the photosynthetic light reaction in cyanobacteria.
                    - paragraph [ref=e1338]:
                      - text: Other critical factors in photobiotechnology, not further addressed here, include temperature and pH control, gas (CO
                      - subscript [ref=e1339]: "2"
                      - text: ", O"
                      - subscript [ref=e1340]: "2"
                      - text: ) mass transfer, strain-specific nutrient supply, water evaporation, harvesting costs, and sterility. Interested readers are referred to relevant reviews (
                      - link "Fabris et al., 2020" [ref=e1341] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B48"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Ullmann and Grimm, 2021" [ref=e1342] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B201"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "van den Berg et al., 2019" [ref=e1343] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B203"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Fernandes et al., 2015" [ref=e1344] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#B53"
                      - text: ).
              - generic [ref=e1345]:
                - heading "4 Conclusions and Perspectives" [level=2] [ref=e1346]: 4 Conclusions and Perspectives
                - paragraph [ref=e1347]:
                  - text: Transhydrogenases and NAD
                  - superscript [ref=e1348]: +
                  - text: kinases have been shown to be promising targets to tune redox metabolism towards an efficient support of NAD(P)H-dependent productive reactions. The introduction of heterologous NAD(P)H generating enzyme systems into whole-cell biocatalysts on the other hand relieves the dependency of target reactions on the cellular redox metabolism and can be suitable for specific biotransformations. Targeted modifications in central carbon metabolism, such as overexpression and deletion of genes encoding critical enzymes or regulators, can effectively enhance redox-dependent production processes. Respective investigations also provide insight into the intricate regulatory network guiding future pathway and genome-scale engineering efforts to further boost productivities. Whereas the PPP has high potential for the optimization of NADPH availability, EDP, TCA cycle, and respective engineering have been shown to be valuable targets to achieve high NAD(P)H regeneration rates. The TCA-cycle also has high value to drive redox reactions depending on metabolites other than NAD(P)H, e.g., α-KG for oxyfunctionalizations catalyzed by α-KG-dependent dioxygenases.
                - paragraph [ref=e1349]:
                  - text: Cyanobacteria and green algae have great potential as whole-cell redox biocatalysts, making use of water and sunlight to provide reduction equivalents. Recent studies showed that introduction of an artificial electron sink could not only benefit from, but also enhance photosynthetic performance. For high light-to-product efficiencies, efficient electron transport in the photosynthetic apparatus is required. Promising strategies include overexpression of limiting elements, e.g., cytochrome b
                  - subscript [ref=e1350]: "6"
                  - text: f, attachment to PSI, and targeted manipulation of protection mechanisms. Further, strategies to avoid intracellular competition are needed, such as attenuating FNR affinity for ferredoxin and thereby making more ferredoxin available for target reactions. Finally, future approaches have to tackle imbalances among redox and energy status of the cells.
              - generic [ref=e1351]:
                - heading "Acknowledgments" [level=2] [ref=e1352]: Acknowledgments
                - paragraph [ref=e1353]: We acknowledge the use of the facilities of the Centre for Biocatalysis (MiKat) at the Helmholtz Centre for Environmental Research, which is supported by European Regional Development Funds (EFRE, Europe funds Saxony) and the Helmholtz Association.
              - generic [ref=e1354]:
                - heading "Author Contributions" [level=2] [ref=e1355]: Author Contributions
                - paragraph [ref=e1356]: ET and BB developed conception and design of the review. ET, AT, and JT wrote the original draft of the manuscript. BB contributed in terms of article structuring and editing. All authors approved the submitted version.
              - generic [ref=e1357]:
                - heading "Conflict of Interest" [level=2] [ref=e1358]: Conflict of Interest
                - paragraph [ref=e1359]: The authors declare that the research was conducted in the absence of any commercial or financial relationships that could be construed as a potential conflict of interest.
              - generic [ref=e1360]:

Any product that may be evaluated in this article, or claim that may be made by its manufacturer, is not guaranteed or endorsed by the publisher.
